BZ, on the label capture sheet: feels like a waste of screen space. A 2x2 grid says the same in a third of the height; the hint moved to the tile's title, where it is there for whoever wants it and in nobody's way. The three-sentence preamble went with it. AND THE RAIL STAYS PUT, after two wrong answers from me. BZ: on PC it goes off the scroll - then: does not stay above the fold on mobile either. ## v2.3.4  ·  2026-09-11 07:59 PM ET

A camera for the levels, and a rail that stays on the screen. BZ asked whether reading levels needed its own camera and I said no, the import photo does both. He asked for one anyway, under Manage, called Photograph fill levels - and he is right: the two are different jobs wearing the same lens. Importing happens once and proposes nine bottles to add; topping up the levels happens on a Sunday, and being shown an add list you dealt with weeks ago is noise on top of the thing you came for. Same read underneath, one call and one cost, and a sheet that says only what it saw through the glass. An empty answer is a RESULT rather than a failure - dark glass reads as nothing on purpose, so a shelf of Islay bottles will often come back with nothing to change, and the sheet says so instead of looking broken. THE RAIL STAYS ON THE SCREEN NOW, after two wrong answers from me. BZ: on PC it goes off the scroll - then, on mobile: does not stay above the fold either. The first version was absolute, which pins to the top of the CONTENT rather than the screen, so it slid away on any page longer than the viewport. The second was sticky, which needs the scrolling ancestor to cooperate, and detailBody sits inside a screen that scrolls itself, so it did nothing at all. Fixed is the one that means what it says: under the header, above the nav, on both. Layer 120, which is above the page and below everything that covers it. AND THE RIGHT BOTTLE, THE RIGHT WAY UP. BZ: empty at the bottom, full at the top, and use the same bottles from the timers. The first pass used a champagne bottle and a TEST TUBE, which is neither the right shape nor a thing that appears anywhere in this app. It is the nav's own bottle now, drawn twice - filled at the top, outline at the foot - so the scale reads as one bottle emptying.

One map now, read wherever a field is named to a person: the fill sheet, the import review that said proof, fin differs, and the lookup log. Anything not listed falls back to itself, which is how a new field gets noticed rather than silently printed raw. AND SHELF TOOLS HAS THREE SECTIONS. ## v2.3.1  ·  2026-09-11 07:21 PM ET

One house, one spelling, one sentence. BZ's bottle screen said This is your only bottle from Angel's Envy, sitting alone among 328 whiskies - above Pour it against Angel's Envy Bourbon Madeira Cask Finish, same house, same strength. He owns many. BZ: feels like a library inconsistency. It is, and it was three faults stacked. FIRST, ELEVEN FUNCTIONS were deciding whether two whiskies share a house and ten of them compared raw strings, so a label read that stored ANGELS ENVY beside Angel's Envy split one house in two. It had been found once and fixed in ONE of them; the other ten were walked past, including the one that feeds the service. All eleven go through L.houseSame now. A text check stands where the twelfth would go, and it found five I had missed - my first pattern only matched dist against dist and said nothing about dist against a plain value, which is the same fault wearing different clothes. SECOND, THE LIBRARY ITSELF. Teaching eleven readers to tolerate bad data is not the same as not having bad data, so the scan reports a distillery filed under two names, names the odd entries, and the editor offers the spelling the rest of the library uses - the same majority rule L.snapHouse applies on the way in, so a fix here and a write there cannot disagree. THIRD, THE SENTENCE WAS STORED. A claim about a shelf is true on the day it is written and the shelf changes underneath it: buy the second Angel's Envy and your only bottle from Angel's Envy stays on screen for ever, wrong for a reason nothing in the app could see. The facts a sentence was written from are recorded beside it now and it is dropped when they stop matching, because an empty card is honest and a confidently wrong one is not. AND THE NAME WRAPS: Pour it against Angel's Envy Bourbon Madeira Cask Finish ran off the right edge of his phone with the button's own border through the middle of the text, because the class was called oneline and a bottle name is not one line. 4076 assertions, 40 consistency checks.

## v2.3.0  ·  2026-09-11 06:38 PM ET

Gone: what you drank, which the shelf has never been able to show. Every other app calls this a Consumed list - Whiskey Shelf moves a bottle there the moment it is marked empty. Here a retired bottle simply vanished: the record was kept, with its reason and its date and who got it, and there was nowhere to read it except by opening the one bottle you already knew about. The shelf already filters by status, so this is a FOURTH STATUS rather than a new screen. One line stood in the way - shelfFilter drops anything you do not currently own before it ever looks at status, and ownedCounts deliberately excludes the gone - so gone gets its own map and its own path past that gate. Open, Sealed and All are untouched, which is asserted rather than hoped: a fourth status that changed the other three would be a regression dressed as a feature. An older test pinned the shelf index to exactly three keys and fired the moment a fourth arrived, which is that check doing its job, because the index is read in several places and a key appearing silently is how one of them ends up reading a map that is not there. TWO THINGS I DID NOT BUILD AND WHY. An in-aisle barcode lookup is on every competitor and is NOT a gap here: the code carries BZ's own words - we don't need the icons, and we were going to remove the barcode everywhere - because in a shop the label reads more than a code does and works on the bottles no database has. Photo a bottle already fills the search box and Shop answers On your shelf with the count, which is the whole question a scanner is asked. And a public collection link needs a Firebase node readable without sign-in and a rules change BZ would have to deploy; that is his decision to make rather than mine to assume. Also in this release, from earlier today: the fill gauge, seven notches, opt-in; running low on Shop; and Another one? when a bottle is retired. 4065 assertions, 39 consistency checks.

## v2.2.2  ·  2026-09-11 06:01 PM ET

How much is left, in seven notches, and a question when a bottle leaves. BZ has turned fill tracking down twice and was right both times: a number you maintain by hand is a chore, and one derived from pour sizes is wrong because not all pours are equal. What changed it was the SHAPE - a slider makes sense if we can put it down the left of the bottle page, and have a shelf setting to enable that feature. So it is vertical, down the left of each open bottle, meant to be held against the actual bottle and matched by eye, and it is OFF until somebody asks for it in Shelf tools. SEVEN NOTCHES. BZ: 10 and 25% increments, no 13% or 73% bs, this is close enough is good enough - then, seeing the scale: I actually meant 10s and 25s but your scale is better, big buckets. Empty, Nearly gone, A quarter left, Half, Three quarters, Barely touched, Full. The two near the ends earn their place because plain quarters can say neither. A dragged finger landing on 73 records 75 and the label never flickers through a number he has said he does not want to see. AND IT LEADS SOMEWHERE, which is the whole reason it is worth setting. BZ: this feature, if enabled, can lead to a Remember to Stock up suggestion on shop, maybe in the form of the wish list. Anything at a quarter or less turns up on Shop above the gaps - a gap is a whisky you have never owned and this is one you are about to stop owning - emptiest first, one tap onto the wishlist. Never for a bottle with a sealed one already on the shelf, because telling somebody to buy what they already own is how a list gets ignored. ANOTHER ONE? asked once when a bottle is retired, however it left - finished, gifted, traded, sold or broken. That is the only moment somebody reliably knows whether they want it back, and the app has always let it pass in silence. Not asked when a spare is on the shelf or it is already on the list. ONE FAULT CAUGHT BY ITS OWN TEST: fillSnap(null) returned 0, because Number(null) is 0 and isFinite(0) is true - so every bottle on the shelf would have read as EMPTY the moment the gauge was switched on. Nothing is not zero. All three state lists agree, showFill declared a setting rather than a collection. 4059 assertions, 39 consistency checks.

## v2.2.1  ·  2026-09-11 05:38 PM ET

A 404 twice is a missing door; once is a blip. Yesterday I taught the app to remember a mode that answers 404 so the second press does not wait five seconds for the same answer. That was right for the case it was built for - a mode that was never deployed answers 404 every time. It is wrong for the case BZ's log actually shows: shelf build failed HTTP 404 at 16:54, again at 17:15, then service answered in 18.8s sent 763KB at 17:18. Same mode, same deployment, sometimes a 404 - a big POST through Apps Script does not always come back. Remembering the FIRST one would have turned a transient failure into a dead feature for the rest of the session, without ever reaching his service again, and he would have had no way to tell the difference from the thing it was meant to fix. A door is dead only after two consecutive 404s now, and any good answer forgets every miss - a mode that has just worked is not missing, whatever it did a minute ago. A genuinely absent mode still costs two waits rather than five; one that hiccuped costs nothing. Both driven in the walk and both proved by putting the old behaviour back and watching three failures name it.

## v2.1.0  ·  2026-09-11 05:25 PM ET

Nine bottles went into the counts and nowhere you could see them. BZ, after photographing a shelf: bottles seem to get into the book counts but not able to find on the shelf and not at the top of the list. Both true and both the same cause. A photographed bottle is added SEALED - correctly, nobody opens nine bottles by photographing them - and the shelf's default filter is OPEN. So nine went in, 365 bottles and 347 whiskies, and the shelf showed him nothing. They were one filter away the whole time and nothing said so. After a photograph the shelf now switches to what was just added: everything, newest owned first, so the work is on the screen rather than somewhere for him to go and find. TWO NEAR MISSES WORTH RECORDING, both caught by measuring rather than by reasoning. S.shelfSort was a key I invented - the shelf reads S.filters.sort, so nothing would have read it and the sort would not have moved. And 'added' SOUNDS like the right sort and is the wrong one: it means the order the catalog holds them, which for a bottle added a second ago is the BOTTOM. Measured on three bottles it put the new one third of three, so choosing it would have made his complaint worse rather than better. 'got' is newest owned first, already existed, and puts it first. On the merges in his log that went into undefined: those ran at 16:23 and the log shows boot AFTER AN UPDATE at 16:24, so they predate resolveLibKey. Checked rather than assumed - a display name resolves to the real key now, and a key whose entry is gone returns null, which the guard turns into could not find that entry rather than a write into the dark.

## v2.0.73  ·  2026-09-11 02:49 PM ET

The Apps Script side has never had a gate. Now it does. BZ: any more instances like this we can improve? The 404 on the flight design came from recap-handler.gs, an old scaffold that defines its OWN doPost - and Apps Script keeps whichever loads last. Its version handles only mode:'recap' and then calls handleExistingLookup_, which exists nowhere in the project, so every other mode fell off the end. Nothing could have caught it: every check ever written for this app reads index.html, and the four files that actually answer it had no checker of any kind. gscheck.js reads them for the two shapes that matter and both are cheap - a name defined twice, where one silently wins and which one depends on file order that nobody controls; and a name called and defined nowhere, which is a ReferenceError the app reports as a broken feature rather than an absent one. It also requires every mode the app can ask for to have a handler AND a line in doPost, so a mode added to the app without a handler fails here rather than in somebody's hand at a bar. Run against his actual project it names the whole fault in four lines: three collisions and the missing function. Scanning the four he is about to paste: clean. FOUR FAULTS IN MY OWN CHECKER FIRST, each found by running it. The mode test looked for mode === 'flight' in a copy of the source that had had every string emptied, so it reported all six modes missing on a file that wires all six. The string stripper worked line by line and four names walked through it - verdict, get_bottle, position and detail, every one inside a Logger line or a column heading. And a local, var pick = function inside runEnrich_, was reported missing because only top-level function declarations were being counted. A checker that cries wolf gets switched off rather than satisfied, which is the whole lesson of tonight.

## v2.0.72  ·  2026-09-11 10:49 AM ET

A door that is not there is asked once. BZ, on designing a flight: it was also kinda slow. Measured on his 325 bottles, the local designer takes between 0.4 and 6.1 milliseconds - it is not slow at all. The wait is the round trip to his Apps Script before it answers 404, which his own log times at 5.0 and 6.3 seconds: five seconds of waiting to be told the door does not exist, and the same five seconds again on the next press. A 404 or a 501 for a given mode is now remembered until the page is reloaded, so the second press is instant. Not an error cached - a door KNOWN to be missing, and a reload clears it, which is exactly what somebody does after deploying, so a fixed deployment is never more than a refresh away. Driven in the walk by pressing a dead mode twice and requiring one round trip, proved by disabling the shortcut and watching it report two.

## v2.0.71  ·  2026-09-11 10:31 AM ET

Wine against sherry is a question about wood, not about Midleton. BZ asked for wine against sherry and got six Spots - Valpolicella, Leoville Barton, Montelena, Quail's Gate, Red Spot, Yellow Spot. All Midleton, all Irish, all 92 proof, and the app announced 15 matched pairs at proof as though that were the achievement. By the rule as written it was correct: finish held the DISTILLERY still, so a cask flight could only ever be one house showing its own range. BZ: it's the second one, vary house, keep category. Right, and it is the better question - what a sherry cask does is a fact about sherry casks rather than about one distillery's warehouse. finish holds the category now; proof, age and grain still hold the house, because those really are questions about one maker's range. On his own shelf a cask flight went from one house to FIVE: Limavady, Spot, Gortinore, Dingle, Bushmills, across sherry, zinfandel, wine, American oak, PX and Bordeaux. AND SIX BOTTLES FROM ONE HOUSE IS NOT A FLIGHT, which nothing had ever said. A matched pair at proof is worth 20 and six bottles at one strength make fifteen of them - three hundred points that swamped everything else in the scorer. House spread is worth something now and a rut costs: the six-Spot flight scores -53 against 85 for four houses. A RECAST WAS SERVING THE SAME SIX WHISKIES. The freshness rule was priced at 25 points against the score, which held until the spread bonus could outbid it. Fewest repeats wins outright now - but that was only half of it: the recast could only ever choose between GROUPINGS, and the day a cask flight started holding the category there was exactly ONE grouping, so the rule had nothing to choose between. pickSpread breaks ties on what the last run poured. Found by a fixture built to catch it, six used and six fresh, which took all six used; the real fixture could not have caught it, because every candidate it offers repeats equally and the assertion passed whichever way the comparison was written. I proved that by inverting the rule and watching it stay green - a test that cannot fail is worse than no test, because it reads like cover.

## v2.0.70  ·  2026-09-11 08:05 AM ET

Leaving the app no longer throws away the read. BZ: if I leave the app while the photo is processing, not close, just leave, the toast says the network was lost and nothing happens. A phone suspends a background tab's fetch; postWithRetry read that as a dropped connection, retried straight into the same suspended state, and gave up - so a shelf read he had waited fifty seconds for was lost because he glanced at something else, and the message blamed the network. It now notices the app was hidden and WAITS for him to come back rather than burning the retry, with a line that says so. The watcher is taken off on all four ways out, or every service call would leave one behind. Worth recording what the harness could NOT tell me: the shelf photo flow works end to end here - photo, Read your shelf, What it found, 2 read, 0 already yours, 2 to add - so when BZ says the process results in nothing, the most likely cause is this same backgrounding, which is not in any build he has. Guessing further without his log would be the thing that has cost us most tonight.

## v2.0.69  ·  2026-09-11 07:56 AM ET

The whole gate in one command, and a line that lied about what it was doing. BZ: when we are photoing our own shelf the toast should not say working out what is yours. Quite - you are standing in front of your own shelf and every bottle on it is yours. That line belongs to the BAR read, where the question really is which of these you own; here the job is the opposite, which of them are already on the shelf and which are new, and it says that now. AND gate.py RUNS ALL NINE STEPS AS ONE CALL. BZ, after a build that went well: that build process was way better, but it seems to me like I needed to prompt you to continue. He did, twice in one run, and the pattern is always the same - I finish a step, report it, and then write a sentence describing what comes NEXT instead of running it. Walk next, 43s is a promise rather than a step. So the steps stop being separate turns: one command runs all nine, prints each as it lands with actual against expected from the median of the last nine runs, and stops at the first failure with sixteen lines of its output. --fast is the seven quick ones at 25s and --slow is the walk and sync at 120s, for when somebody would rather watch it than wait for it. The trade is worth saying plainly: one call means 145 seconds of nothing on screen and then everything at once, while a call per step shows progress and gives me nine chances to stall. Proved by breaking a check on purpose - it stopped at audit in five seconds and named both the undocumented control and the stale lock file.

## v2.0.67  ·  2026-09-11 12:31 AM ET

Sync runs in slices, so ninety seconds of silence becomes four reports. BZ: is there a way to chunk up the big ones? For sync yes - its seventeen scenarios are independent by construction, each with its own page and its own seeded database, which is the whole design. SYNC_FROM and SYNC_TO pick a one-based inclusive range; absent, everything runs exactly as before and the full run is 75.9s against 76 expected. THE WALK IS THE OPPOSITE AND MUST NOT BE CUT. Its forty-three steps are a SEQUENCE: step thirty depends on what step twelve did to the page, which is rule 30e and the reason the walk catches what the unit suite cannot. Chopping it would break the property that makes it worth forty-three seconds. THREE THINGS HAD TO GIVE WAY, each found by running it rather than reasoning about it. A skipped scenario returning {} was not enough, because a check's ARGUMENTS are evaluated before check can stand down and after.account.bottles threw first - so a skipped scenario returns a ghost that absorbs property reads, calls and iteration. The ghost then needed a primitive value, because a few assertions are raw ifs that concatenate or compare the result directly. And guarding check() was still not enough: one of those raw ifs read r.length as 0 and reported Diagnostics drew nothing on a scenario that never ran - so the guard moved to where failures are RECORDED, which is the one place all of them pass through. AND POLLING A BACKGROUND RUN DOES NOT WORK HERE, which is worth writing down because it looked like it did: a process started with nohup is killed when the call that launched it returns, and reading its half-written output looked exactly like the walk hanging on the guest step. It was not hanging. The walk ran 43.2s against 43 expected immediately afterwards.

You can see what differs, and the identical pairs finally merge. BZ, looking at two rows printed identically with no Merge button between them: no clear way to understand what looks like a match. Two rows printed identically resolve to the SAME entry when you look them up by name, because a lookup by name can only ever find one of them - so the guard refused to merge a key with itself, which was right, against a pair that was real. The gate is nine harnesses and two take over a minute; run as one command it prints nothing anybody can look at until it finishes, and a wait with no evidence in it is indistinguishable from a broken session. So: say what is about to run and how long it takes, from gatetime.py which keeps thirty runs and expects the MEDIAN OF NINE; run it in groups that match the timing - seven fast steps in one call at 24s, the walk alone at 43s, sync alone at 77s; report actual against expected the moment it lands; and NEVER announce a step without running it in the same turn, because writing running the walk now and not running it is worse than silence - it reads as progress and there is none. Sync has nine runs on record and is the first step with a real expectation: 77.3 seconds. The rest need a few more, and until then the tool says so rather than inventing a number. ON GOING FASTER, the honest position: the seventeen sync scenarios are independent by construction and could run concurrently, but the script interleaves its checks between them, so it is a restructure of six hundred lines rather than a change. A merge that reported success and deleted nothing. BZ's log, three times in ninety seconds: merged heaven hill grain to glass wheated bourbon into heaven_hill_grain_to_glass_straight_wheated_bourbon, then library scan: 3 kind(s) in 434. Merged, said so, and the count never moved - 434 before and 434 after, three times over. The merges that worked that night all used underscore keys on both sides; the two that did nothing used the printed NAME, spaces and capitals and all. A library entry is not always filed under libKey of its own name - an import, a rename or an older publish can leave it anywhere - so a key worked out from what is on the screen points at a node that is not there, and a DELETE AGAINST A NODE THAT IS NOT THERE SUCCEEDS. That is the worst shape a bug can have: it reports success, and three presses later the row is still on the list. L.resolveLibKey takes the key we were given if it is really there, else the normalised form, else the entry whose name matches, and null when none of them is - at which point the merge says it could not find the entry rather than writing into the dark. Caught by the two-doors check the same night, on its author: resolveLibKey calls libKey, which is exactly the shape that check hunts for, and it is allowed by name with the reason. BZ cleared a region on one half of a pair and the save was refused - the library already holds Yellowstone Special Finishes Collection Rum Cask - when he had not touched the name. A mash bill is a list, not a proximity puzzle - and the scan stops reporting correct bottles. BZ spot-checked thirteen bills the scan called wrong: some actually do add up to 100%. They did. The parser looked for a number within fourteen characters of a grain word, which is how a bill is usually written and not how they are actually written. Every failure was the same mistake - guessing which number belongs to which grain by how CLOSE they sit. 96% soft red winter wheat put the number 24 characters away. 51% yellow dent corn did the same. 27% wheat, 62% malted wheat was deduped to one wheat because "a grain named twice is one grain" was wrong. And 70% corn, 25% malted rye read the malted as MALT and left a naked rye with no percentage against it - malting is something you do TO a grain, not a different grain. It reads SEGMENTS now: split on the separators people use, and each piece gives one percentage and one grain however many describing words sit between them. Every bill BZ handed me sums to 100. 95% rye alone still flags, which is the Angel's Envy case he found by searching - a real gap, and the one the Look it up button is for. ## v2.0.46  ·  2026-09-10 05:47 PM ET

What they liked travels with them, and the comments stop describing code that is gone. BZ: if someone says they like a PX finished scotch, would we hold PX into the location ladder? Smoke? Proof level? Yes to all three, as an ORDER rather than a gate. A gate would empty a rung the moment the shelf held no other PX Islay and the ladder would step outward to a country it should not have reached, for the wrong reason and saying the wrong thing to the guest. The rung is a distance in PLACE; the cask, the smoke and the strength are what make two whiskies taste alike whatever the place, so they decide who leads inside it. THE CASK FIRST, because it is the loudest thing in the glass and the one a guest names unprompted - the exact finish beats the family, PX to PX before PX to Oloroso, because they said PX and meant it. My first version made Pedro Ximenez its own family, which put Oloroso as far away as rum: a PX drinker with no PX on the shelf got anything at all rather than the next sherry along. SMOKE SECOND, and it costs more when it is wrong - handing an unpeated malt to a peat drinker is not a near miss, whatever the distillery. L.peatLevel already decided this from the house and the name and nothing new was invented. STRENGTH LAST AND ONE-WAY. BZ: proof should be >=. Handing somebody who drinks at cask strength an 80 proof is a downgrade they notice in the first sip; handing an 86 drinker a 96 is at worst a bigger drink. Measured on his own shelf: an Oloroso+PX Aberlour leads down the road with three PX bottles and across the pond with Redbreast PX; a peated Islay gives peated Obans down the road and Connemara across it; A'Bunadh at 121.8 leads with cask strength rather than an 80. THE LONGEST COMMENT IN THE FILE WAS REASONING FOR CODE NOBODY COULD CALL. ## v2.0.38  ·  2026-09-10 03:05 PM ET

One shelf read, used by both places that read a shelf. BZ: reuse!!! keep the same things the same. Two copies existed and had ALREADY DRIFTED, which is the thing he is shouting about rather than a tidiness point. Both shrank the photographs, posted mode=shelf, read the answer, ran L.shelfSeen over it and named what came back - and only ONE checked the daily lookup cap before spending a call, and only one reported a service error rather than reporting a blank shelf. A web page is not data, and the app now says so instead of quoting a parser. BZ, testing a shelf photo: no bottles mentioned by name, 404 error on the screen. The bottle names never appeared for the same reason: the response never parsed, so the reveal had nothing to walk. Checking res.ok would not have caught it either - Apps Script answers 200 and puts its error page in the BODY, so the only way to know is to read the body and look at it. There is one reader now and all four call sites use it: the label read, both shelf reads and the recap. When the body starts with a bracket it is parsed; when it starts with a DOCTYPE the message says the address returned a web page rather than data, and names the three things that actually cause it - a stale deployment, which is rule 25e and the most likely; an address pointing at the editor instead of the deployed web app; or the script throwing and Apps Script serving its own error page. When it is neither, the first hundred and twenty characters of whatever DID arrive are quoted, because Exception: mode not handled is worth more than any sentence I could write about it. The head of every unreadable response goes into the log too, so the next report carries the evidence without anybody having to ask. AND THE TIMING FROM YESTERDAY EARNED ITS KEEP ON ITS FIRST RUN: service answered in 49.3s, sent 342KB. ## v2.0.15  ·  2026-09-09 11:08 PM ET

The keyboard is not a smaller screen, which is what the tab bar reports actually were. A real iPhone finally produced the evidence: Kevrin's phone, Safari, v2.0.12, photographed. Tapping the search box on Shop put the whole nav bar in the MIDDLE of the page, above a blank half-screen, with the keyboard below it. That is not a bar covering the bottom of a page. It is a bar parked halfway up one, and every report of the bar blocking content is explained by it. THE MEASUREMENT WAS FOLLOWING THE KEYBOARD DOWN. visualViewport.height is the right thing to watch for a TOOLBAR, which is why it was chosen, and it also halves when the keyboard opens. The column was then sized to the space above the keyboard and the nav, being its last child, sat at the bottom of that. The same phone's boot lines say everything else is healthy - nav 569-627 in 627, and 611-669 in 669, bottom exactly equal to the viewport both times - so this is one case needing one exception rather than another layout. A keyboard is not the screen getting smaller: the page keeps its height and the browser scrolls the focused field into view, which is what every other site does. Anything much shorter than the layout viewport is a keyboard rather than a toolbar, because a toolbar is tens of pixels and a keyboard is hundreds, so the last good height stands until it goes away. ONE ANSWER TO HOW TALL IS THE PAGE, because navSelfHeal was about to become the second: with the keyboard up it would have seen the bar below the visual viewport, called it wrong, and put back exactly the fault this fixes. Both read appHeightNow, and the self-heal stands down entirely while a keyboard is open - correcting that would be correcting the fix. REPRODUCED AND PROVED. visualViewport forced to 380 against a 669 layout, the way iOS does it, and app-h holds at 669 through both the measurement and the self-heal. The walk drives the same numbers on every ship, and removing the exception on purpose makes it report the page shrank to 445px instead of staying 780px - the fault in the photograph, in numbers. Worth saying plainly after three builds of guessing at it: the self-correction shipped at v2.0.11 and the clearance at v2.0.11 were both reasonable and neither was this. One screenshot from a phone nobody here owns settled in one round what inference had not settled in three. 3791 assertions, 30 consistency checks, 26 audit checks, 21 screens, sync, render, two tabs and the walk all green.

## v2.0.12  ·  2026-09-09 10:17 PM ET

Being findable publishes your shelf, because otherwise why be findable. BZ, looking at two buddies who had both accepted and whose rows both read their shelf has not arrived yet: being findable should include your shelf, otherwise why be findable. Right, and the gap was real rather than cosmetic. A grant is written the instant somebody accepts; the SNAPSHOT at view/uid was only ever written by their own device on its next SAVE. Between accepting and next adding a bottle there is nothing for you to read, and that gap can be days - two of BZ's three buddies were sitting in it. NOBODY ELSE CAN CLOSE IT, which is worth stating because it sounds like something an admin should be able to fix. Their bottles live in their own node and the snapshot exists precisely BECAUSE the raw shelf is private; an admin cannot generate it either. But their device can, and it has no reason to wait: the read rule on view/uid is the GRANT, so writing the snapshot earlier changes nothing about who may see it. It offers rather than exposes. So it is published whenever there is anybody it could be for and nothing is there - on load, on accepting, and on turning findable on. Somebody who accepted last week and has not touched the app since publishes their shelf the next time they merely OPEN it. The signature guard that stops a save rewriting an unchanged snapshot is bypassed for that first write, or a device whose shelf has not changed since its last push would go on publishing nothing. AND THE LIGHT STOPPED CONTRADICTING THE ROW. BZ noticed the column head said YOU SEE THEIRS with a green light above the words their shelf has not arrived yet. The light was showing the GRANT and the text the SHELF - two things split apart deliberately at v1.9.23 and then left disagreeing a line apart. Three states now: green for granted and here, AMBER for granted with nothing to read yet, red for nothing back. The walk asserts all three, including that a shelf which has not arrived does not show as arrived. Amber is a short window rather than a state to sit in now, which is the point of the rest of this build. 3861 assertions, 30 consistency checks, 26 audit checks, 21 screens, sync, render, two tabs and the walk all green.

## v2.0.10  ·  2026-09-09 04:13 PM ET

The scan acts, and a withdrawal finally travels. BZ asked the question that should have come before any of this: will everyone's shelf update? A CORRECTION did - a changed row lands in S.base on the next library check and rebuildCatalog picks it up. A REMOVAL and a RENAME did not, and both failed silently, which is the worst way for a shared list to be wrong. Nothing anywhere deleted from a device's copy of the catalog, and the incremental fetch asks for rows NEWER than a stamp - a deletion has no row to send - so an entry removed from the library stayed on every existing device for ever. The tombstone stopped it being re-added; nothing ever took it away. And the library is keyed by NAME, so renaming wrote a new key: devices gained the new product, kept the old one, and a bottle pointing at the old key stayed pointing there. The duplicate being fixed, reproduced on every device, beside the original. Both lists travel now and are applied on every check. A RENAME MOVES THE BOTTLES, because a bottle is somebody's and losing it to a spelling correction would be the worst outcome available - and a device that has not yet received the new entry WAITS rather than moving a bottle to a key nothing knows. A REMOVAL DROPS THE PRODUCT AND LEAVES THE BOTTLE, counted and said in the log: a bottle whose whisky has gone is already named by the diagnostics, and quietly deleting somebody's bottle because a librarian tidied up is not a repair. AND THE SCAN IS A TOOL RATHER THAN A REPORT. BZ: what happened after we scan the library? Nothing did - it listed names and you closed it. Every finding carries the ENTRY it is about now, so every row opens that entry and a correction is one tap from the finding. Every row can also be LEFT, which matters more than it sounds: two of the four things the scan finds on BZ's own catalog are CORRECT as stored - Southern Comfort really is 70 proof and is a liqueur, Macaloney's really is new make and the name misleads - and a check that reports the same two for ever is one somebody learns to skim, which is how a check stops working. Leaving one is written where every admin reads it rather than on one device, because it is a judgment about a shared row, and it is kept per finding AND per entry so leaving one bottle alone does not silence the same fault on another. The id is escaped going out and unescaped coming back, because a library key can carry a dot and Firebase will not take one in a key - unmatched, the entry would keep reporting and Leave it would look broken rather than say so. 3861 assertions, 30 consistency checks, 26 audit checks, 21 screens, sync, render, two tabs and the walk all green.

## v2.0.9  ·  2026-09-09 03:57 PM ET

Scan the library, and Spin sits in the middle. SCAN THE LIBRARY FOR INCONSISTENCIES, which BZ asked for: the library is the one thing everybody reads, it is written by several people and by a lookup service, and nothing had ever looked at it as a whole - the only checks it had were on a single entry as it arrived. FIVE OF THE CHECKS ALREADY EXISTED. L.importAudit finds the same whisky under two names, a proof or a size stranded in a name, one house spelled two ways, and entries too bare to read; it takes a keyed map and never touches its bottles argument, so it runs on library rows unchanged and that is asserted rather than assumed, because it reuses that is exactly the claim that turns out to be false later. ## v2.0.4  ·  2026-09-09 02:31 PM ET

A place holds a type, a shelf has a job. BZ on the storage model: a cabinet, for me and others, tends to be type specific - one bookshelf is Scotch, Irish and international while the other is Bourbon, Rye and American single malt. And within one, we should know if there is a TOP shelf for special bottles and a WELL shelf for common pours. Neither was in the model, so a plan could put Laphroaig on the bourbon bookcase, be arithmetically perfect, and be useless to the person standing in the room. TYPES BELONG TO THE PLACE, because that is where the decision lives - you do not put one Islay on the bourbon shelf, you keep a bookcase for Scotch. A unit that names nothing takes whatever the named ones did not, which means only the particular places have to be described. Measured on BZ's own shelf against his own furniture, 344 bottles across two typed bookcases: zero Scotch or Irish on the American one and zero bourbon or rye on the Scotch one. ROLES BELONG TO THE SHELF, because top and well are about reach rather than category. A top shelf takes the ones worth showing somebody - ranked by how hard they are to replace, with price breaking the tie, both measured off fields every entry in the catalog already carries. A well shelf takes what you pour without thinking, cheapest first, and anything scarce is disqualified outright rather than merely ranked low: the well is not the bottom of a list, it is a different question. Both fill BEFORE the grouping runs, from the whole of what that unit holds, because a top shelf that gets whatever was left over is not a top shelf - and that is asserted, not assumed: nothing left on an ordinary shelf outranks what sits on the top one. The grouping is untouched. L.shelfPlan still reads Scotch by region and American by house on the shelves with no job, and still refuses to break a house up to save three spaces. AND THE SETUP GRID IS TIGHTER, as asked: cells were 84px with a full-height field in each, so five shelves filled a phone. 62px and a shallower field puts a bookcase on one line and the whole room on a screen, with each cell now carrying the shelf's job as well as its size - cycled on one press, because three states do not need a menu. 3828 assertions, 29 consistency checks, 26 audit checks, 21 screens, sync, render, two tabs and the walk all green.

There is a Sharing check in Diagnostics now that reads all four records and repairs what it can, and the Buddies tab draws one row per person with both directions on it - your switch, their light - rather than two lists of different people wearing one heading. A scan nothing runs is not a check, it is a file. AND THE THINGS THE APP CLAIMS ABOUT A SHELF COME FROM THAT SHELF. What 2.0.0 does not mean: the app is not finished, the road trip planner has never been designed, the fill has not run, and the candidate finder has still never put a bottle in anybody's hands. ## v1.9.40  ·  2026-09-09 11:31 AM ET

The gift list goes as the list, or as a link. BZ: send wish list via text, but link or content. Both, because they are different messages rather than two ways of sending one, and which is right depends on who is receiving it. THE LIST arrives readable in the message. It can be read in a shop without tapping anything, forwarded to somebody else, or read down the phone to a relative who will never open a link, and it still carries no prices - a gift list with prices on it tells somebody what to spend, which is a different message again. THE LINK is one line, and what it opens can say more than a text politely can. It needs no account and nothing installed at the other end, because the names travel inside the link itself: the payload rides in the URL FRAGMENT, which is never sent to a server, so a relative opening it on a borrowed phone sees the list and no part of it was ever transmitted anywhere. Percent-encoded before base64, because btoa throws on anything outside Latin-1 and that is exactly the bottles worth asking for - Fèis Ìle survives the round trip and is asserted. IT IS A SNAPSHOT AND IT SAYS SO. A live link would mean the list being readable by anybody holding the URL, and every read in this database requires an account, so the choice was a snapshot or a rules change that makes a wishlist public. Snapshot, and the date it was taken is shown when it opens, because a gift list that silently went stale in somebody's messages is worse than one that admits its date. NAMES ONLY. Not the prices, which the short list has always omitted, and not the reasons - which are notes the owner wrote for themselves - both asserted by checking they do not appear in the URL. THE READING END REFUSES RUBBISH RATHER THAN THROWING, because it runs on a stranger's phone from an address anybody can edit: not base64, valid base64 that is not a gift, an empty name list and a forty-bottle list are all handled, the last by capping at twenty rather than trusting what arrived. Closing it clears the address, or a reload would reopen somebody else's list over the top of your own shelf. 3808 assertions, 28 consistency checks, 26 audit checks, walk green, sync green.

## v1.9.39  ·  2026-09-09 11:23 AM ET

A calendar is pours, not bottles. BZ, when the advent calendar came up again: calendars are POURS, not bottles. That is the whole thing, and it turned a backlog feature into two lines and a rule. A calendar is twenty-four samples somebody drinks and never owns, so nothing about it belongs on a shelf - and the machinery for a whisky you drank without owning has existed since the away pour: a typed name, no bottle created, the glass logged against what you have tasted. What was missing was the WORD for it. An advent calendar and a sample are sources you can pick where you would pick a bar. AND A SOURCE THAT STANDS ALONE HAS TO SURVIVE HAVING NO NAME. A bar with no name is nothing worth keeping - somewhere is not a place - so parsePlace dropped an unnamed source entirely, and a December of calendar pours would have read as away pours from nowhere. L.bottleFrom already had this exact rule for a lottery and an auction, where winning one IS the story, so the same rule applies here rather than a new one: a calendar and a sample keep their kind with no name, everything else still needs one, and the list of what may stand alone is two entries long and asserted. It also has to SAY so - a fact kept in the record and shown as an empty string is the same as not keeping it - so an unnamed calendar reads as An Advent Calendar, while a named one reads as its name, because next December is a different calendar. A calendar pour of something you DO own is still an ordinary pour of your own bottle with the source kept, which is the case that would have quietly created a duplicate had it gone the other way. THE ENTRY SAT IN THE BACKLOG AS A FEATURE FOR TWO DAYS because nobody asked what it was. It was listed under Occasions beside advent-with-others, next to tasting nights and trips, as though it were something to build. One question from BZ - what IS a calendar, in this app's terms - collapsed it into a vocabulary gap. Worth remembering the next time a backlog entry names a thing rather than a behavior. 3787 assertions, 28 consistency checks, 26 audit checks, walk green, sync green.

## v1.9.38  ·  2026-09-09 10:49 AM ET

One reader of a grain bill, and the loop said once. BZ ruled on the finding the backlog had flagged as the one that will bite: the real bill wins, one source answers. It had already half-moved - L.mashbill consults the printed bill before falling back to the name - so the duplication was no longer between a name-reader and a bill-reader. It was WORSE: L.mashFromBill and L.mashShape were two readers of the SAME evidence, the grain percentages, in different vocabularies, neither aware of the other. They agreed only while no entry carried a bill, and the fill would have made them disagree in public: a whisky the bottle screen called rye-forward is `rye` everywhere else in the app. mashShape is deleted. The bottle screen calls mashFromBill directly, which is older, has the fuller vocabulary - four-grain, malt and corn as well - and is what mashbill already used, so the survivor answers in the words its existing callers expect. That is the half of rule 30d that stops a merge breaking what it tidied. AND THE MERGE MOVED ONE BOUNDARY, TOWARD THE LAW, which is asserted rather than left to be discovered. 80% corn is the legal floor for corn whiskey, so the survivor calls an 80/10/10 bill corn where the deleted reader said nothing at all. The test that caught it was mine, written from the function being deleted rather than the one surviving, and 79% is now pinned beside 80% so the line cannot drift. GOOD, BETTER, BEST, DONE SUBTLY. BZ asked in September whether to hint at a ranking - label pics best, barcode better, typing good - and ruled today: do subtly. A ranking is the wrong shape and the backlog said why: it mixes correctness with cost, a barcode is only cheap once somebody has TAUGHT the pairing, half a shelf never appears on a retail listing, and above all they are not competitors - reading a label is what teaches the barcode, so ranking them hides the only thing worth knowing. Two callers deciding one thing separately is how this app has been bitten twice this week, and it is not being written a third time (rule 30d). UNDO IS A REAL UNDO. The tombstone is lifted in the SAME write that puts the row back, so a restored entry is genuinely restored rather than a row sitting in the library that both automatic paths still treat as deleted. Delete, un-delete, delete is an ordinary sequence and it has to end deleted - the merge honors tombstones over the map, so a stale lift would have quietly canceled the second deletion on the next sync. ## v1.9.29  ·  2026-09-09 08:36 AM ET

The furniture somebody actually owns. BZ, asked what his set-up is: two 5-shelf bookcases, two one-shelf areas, two shelves at the bar for well brands and flavored plus vodka, tequila and gin, and one cabinet for backups. Fifteen places in six pieces of furniture, and the old organizer could describe none of it. It was a textarea of numbers - 30, 30, 24 - and a plan that said Shelf 7. Shelf 7 means nothing when you are standing in front of a bookcase in another room, and nothing in that model could know that two of those shelves hold gin. Three things his description makes true, and the third changes the arithmetic rather than the labels. FURNITURE HAS A NAME, so a place is a named unit with shelves and a plan says Bookcase A, shelf 2. A PLACE HAS A PURPOSE: a unit is whisky on show, backups, or not whisky at all, and a bar shelf marked not whisky is never planned into - though it is still named on the inventory, because there are bottles on it and the person checking the house wants to walk past it. AND A BACKUP CABINET IS A STATUS RATHER THAN A PLACE. BZ buys a spare of anything he wants to keep having, so the cabinet holds SEALED bottles, which means placement reads a bottle's status and not only its category, and a whisky owned twice lives in two places at once - one open on a shelf, one sealed in the cabinet. That is two rows on the inventory and it is correct: there really are two bottles to find. With no backup place described the spares stay on the shelves rather than being dropped for want of somewhere to put them. THE PLACEMENT ITSELF IS UNCHANGED. L.shelfPlan keeps its rules - group Scotch by region and American by house, never break a house up to save three spaces, collect the lone bottles at the end - and is run once per pool with that pool's capacities, then the flat shelf numbers are put back on the furniture they came from (rule 2). A CAPACITY GRID replaces the textarea: one box per shelf, top to bottom, with quick buttons named after the things BZ described, because a bookcase is one press rather than six blank rows. AND AN INVENTORY TO CHECK OFF, which he asked for: every bottle in the order you would find it, under the place and shelf it belongs on, a tap to tick it, and the ticks remembered - three hundred bottles is an evening, not a sitting. A tick left behind by a bottle that has since left the shelf is ignored rather than counted, so the number cannot drift. Measured on BZ's shipped 344-bottle shelf against his stated furniture: 404 spaces across 15 shelves, 325 on show, 19 sealed spares to the cabinet, the bar left alone, one bottle that will not fit and is listed rather than hidden. The furniture SYNCS, beside shelfCaps and for the same reason - one description of one room, read from a desk and from a phone in front of the shelf - and it is named a scalar on purpose in the merge check, because a union of two descriptions would invent furniture that does not exist and then plan bottles onto it. The ticks stay on the device: merging two half-finished walks round a house would invent a check nobody did. 3605 assertions, 27 consistency checks, 26 audit checks, walk green, sync green.

The workaround stops scaling with the user base. Named in the morning review as the one performance fault introduced rather than inherited, and fixed here. TWO TIERS. People this account already has a relationship record with - both sharing directions and whoever is on screen - are read by name always and are never subject to a cap, because there are as many of them as you have buddies and they are already in hand with no network read of their own. The directory sweep is second, capped at 40, and says in the log when it caps and how many it did not probe, so a grant to somebody not otherwise known can be MISSED and that is stated rather than hidden. The cap is chosen and not measured, and is marked as such in the file (rule 26b). The stats collection is gone from the candidates entirely: it is every account that exists, it is admin-only, and it was the term that grew with the user base. AND ONCE PER SESSION RATHER THAN ONCE PER RENDER. The answer only changes when a grant does, so it is cached for ninety seconds and cleared by every path that grants, revokes, declines or signs in. ## v1.9.24  ·  2026-09-09 01:29 AM ET

Two readers of one node, printed side by side. BZ, on the third round of the same picture: closer but. The Buddies tab drew one row while the Sharing check listed three people and said all three could see his shelf, on the same account at the same moment. Both cannot be right, and there is no way to tell which from the outside, so this build stops theorising and prints the evidence. THE FOUR RECORDS ARE NAMED AND SHOWN RAW under every person on the Sharing card - shares/you/them, sharedWith/them/you, shares/them/you, sharedWith/you/them - each as yes, no or refused, by its real path. Refused is kept distinct from no on purpose: a read the rules turned down is not an absence, and treating it as one is how a repair button would delete somebody's access on a guess. AND THE TWO READERS ARE COMPARED HEAD TO HEAD. The Buddies tab builds its rows from fbSharesMine, which enumerates the CHILDREN of shares/you; the Sharing card reads each child by name. Those are different operations against the same node and they are now run side by side, both counts printed, and the line turns red and says so when they disagree. That comparison IS the diagnosis: if the enumeration finds fewer than the individual reads, the tab is being lied to about who it shares with, and if it finds more, the card is. Rule 13c says never reason about data I cannot see - my library, my deployed build, my log, my device - and this is the build that stops doing it. A build that logs its decision per item settles in one round what inference does not settle in six, and this is the third round on one screen. SHARED.shelves is whose shelf you actually hold, and loadSharedShelves filled it by reading the grants and then fetching each shelf - and returned early, silently, on anybody whose snapshot was missing. ## v1.9.22  ·  2026-09-09 01:16 AM ET

A share is four records and they can disagree. BZ, with his own account and Not Smoky Bill's open side by side: green green on his side and green red on mine. Both apps were reporting honestly, which is what made it worth finding. Letting somebody see your shelf writes TWO records - shares/owner/them, which is the grant the database rules actually enforce access from, and sharedWith/them/owner, the index that lets the other person's app know the grant exists - and fbAccept writes them one after the other rather than in a single update. So an accept can half-land: the grant is there, the index is not, the owner's app reads green and the viewer's app reads red, permanently, and nothing anywhere notices because each side is correctly reporting the half it can reach. THE SHARING CHECK, in Diagnostics and deliberately not admin-only, because the person who cannot see a shelf is the one who notices. It reads all four records for everybody either side holds a record about - found from both directions, so somebody whose index went missing is still found through the grant - says which of the two grants are whole and which half is missing, and repairs it. Two of those four were unreadable from any account until today: the rules published on 2026-09-09 give an admin read on shares and sharedWith for anybody and write on both, which is what turns this from a guess into a measurement and makes the repair possible at all. A record belonging to the other person can only be rewritten by them or by an admin, and the button says so rather than failing quietly. THE GRANT IS THE TRUTH AND THE REPAIR ALWAYS MOVES TOWARD IT. An index with no grant behind it is REMOVED, never turned into access nobody granted - a repair button that can hand out a shelf is a worse bug than the one it fixes. And a refused read is reported as refused rather than as an absence, because repairing an absence you cannot actually see would delete somebody's access on a guess. AN ACCOUNT WITH NO DISPLAY NAME IS STILL SOMEBODY. BZ, on an admin row reading (no name): can we put name or email on this. Four of the five rows were already named, because every account records its own display name on each sync; the fifth never set one, and a sign-in address is the only thing an account always has. It is recorded from Auth rather than typed, blank when the provider does not carry one, and it stands in as the name when there is none and sits under the name when there is. Who can see it, checked rather than assumed: that node is readable by an admin and by the account itself, nobody else, and it needed no rules change to add - which is why that sentence is written down beside the code rather than left to be worked out later. An address appears only once that account has opened this version, which the screen now says. App use carries the sharing check and rule 9z, BZ's standing instruction that App use is updated with every build. 3537 assertions, 27 consistency checks, 26 audit checks, walk green. NOT RUN THIS BUILD: sync.js, for the same reason as the last eleven.

## v1.8.36  ·  2026-09-06 11:30 PM ET

Three ways to lose a removal, a search over the pour list, and the bench can be edited. BZ asked for an audit of every data operation against the sync. It found three ways to lose a removal, all one root: merging two copies of a list can only ever ADD, so anything taken out on one device is put back by the other. Unstar a favorite and the star returns. Delete a custom flight or a pour and it returns. A list of removals is the one thing a union handles correctly, because it only ever grows - so removals are recorded rather than implied, and every merge honors them: the live listener, the first load and the read before a push. A star added elsewhere still arrives; a star removed here stays removed. Also audited and found safe: deadGaps, tastingsSeen, libLedger, askStats, bottleSaid and upcs only ever grow, so a union is exactly right for them, and edits and custom removals were already covered by the deleted tombstone. Then the flight editor. BZ: adding a pour manually needs a search, it is a long list - or we could reduce it by the flight's characteristics. The reducing already happened and was not enough: PROOF IS NOT A SCORE offers 30 that fit and 289 that do not, and WHY IS THIS ONE HOT offers 123 that fit, which is not a list anybody scrolls on a phone. ## v1.8.35  ·  2026-09-06 11:14 PM ET

Unsent is a question about a KEY, not about a device. BZ removed a bottle from the wishlist on his phone and it stayed on his desktop. On a local win the load skipped every key not in the merge list, and his desktop always wins locally because it always has some unsent change - so the account's wishlist was never taken. The rule exists to protect unsent work, which is right; it was protecting keys this device had never touched, which is not. Asked per key it is simply true: a key whose local value still matches what this device last pushed is a key this device has not changed, so the account's copy is the truth, and a key that differs is unsent work that must not be overwritten by the copy it is about to replace. That is BZ's own sentence applied at the right grain - the database is the source when connected, and only work done here that has not gone yet is exempt. The test caught a second fault while proving the first: the live listener was DISCARDING unsent local work for any key not in the merge list, taking the account's copy over a name typed a second earlier. Same root, opposite direction, and it would have quietly eaten edits. Both paths ask the same question now. Also from BZ: with the words on the page the toast is not needed - it existed because the form was being destroyed and the message had nowhere to land, and now that the form comes back with the line in it and the cursor in the name field, a second copy over the top is noise. And a lookup that succeeds now records WHICH fields came back and which did not, named by bottle: lookup Penelope Wheated Bourbon filled proof, sub - missing dist, age, fin, msrp. Driving a full answer through fills dist and msrp correctly, so a gap is the service not sending it - and a partial answer and a complete one looked identical in the log until now. 2926 assertions.

## v1.8.29  ·  2026-09-06 08:22 PM ET

The tabs. Measured, not guessed. BZ's own log, from his Android after an update: boot AFTER AN UPDATE, nav 783-841 in 785. The bar laid out with its bottom 56 points BELOW the fold, which is why the tabs were gone. Twenty seconds later, a clean boot on the same device and the same build: nav 727-785 in 785, flush. dvh is resolved once, against the viewport as it is at that instant. On the reload that follows an update the browser toolbar is hidden, dvh resolves to the taller value, the toolbar comes back, and dvh does not recompute - so the page is 56 points too tall for ever, which is exactly the safe-area inset, and the last child of the column is pushed off the screen. Closing and reopening fixed it because that is a fresh layout with the toolbar already in place. The height is now MEASURED from the real viewport on load, on resize, on orientation change, on visualViewport resize, and twice more after the toolbar has had time to settle. The detector was also throwing Invalid element or state on the first frame of every scan, because detect was called before the video had dimensions; it waits for a frame. Neither device updated automatically and the diagnostics said service worker false - an install that fails takes no update with it and reports nowhere, which is how a device quietly stops updating for ever. index.html is the only file that can fail the install now, the root path is cached best-effort beside it, and a failed install posts its reason to every open page, which writes it into the log. ## v1.8.28  ·  2026-09-06 07:56 PM ET

The shelf reads before it writes, and so does the log. BZ: I am really worried the shelf will fall prey to the same issues. He was right, and it had the fault already. A push wrote S.bottles straight over the account, so a bottle added on another device between this device's load and this device's push was erased. The load merges and the live listener merges; the WRITE did not, and a write that replaces undoes both of them. Written as a failing test first: the account gains a bottle while this device has one of its own to send, and both must survive. It came back with two of three - the other device's bottle gone, exactly the fault, reproduced before it was fixed. Read, merge, then write, for the record lists only. A setting is a setting: last writer wins is right for a display name and wrong for a collection. The merge costs a read rather than a payload, so a one-bottle correction is still 56 bytes. The same fault was still live in the log write, which is what BZ noticed first: the phone's barcode lines were only in the phone's log because a bare set replaced the account's copy with whichever device wrote last. The write was destroying what it was meant to join. It merges first now, and the assertion holds in both directions. One thing worth recording about the fix rather than the fault: the guard against re-reading is a PARAMETER, not a flag on FB. Clearing a shared flag before the second call meant the second call read again, and again, for ever - the read is what schedules the next push, so a flag the recursion can see reset is not a guard at all. That cost a two hundred second timeout to notice. 3 new assertions, 2926 total.

The instrument was there and buried. BZ sent a log with no boot line in it, and the boot lines were being written - they were sorted underneath a wall of yesterday. ## v1.8.24  ·  2026-09-06 06:40 PM ET

The database is the source when connected. BZ: we have a real time database that must be the source when connected - if and only if a device does work in a disconnected state should it contribute async, otherwise direct writing. He is right, and it is a different design from the one in the file. What was here treated the device and the account as two peers holding rival copies and arbitrated between them with stamps. That is a design for peers, not for clients of a live database, and it is where every sync fault of today came from: local winning against a newer account, a push clobbering the other device, two logs staying separate. Three bugs, one wrong model. Written as three failing tests first, because this is the one part of the app that must never lose data. A remote change arrives without a reload. A write while connected reaches the account at once. Work done offline contributes and neither device's bottle is lost. All three failed against the old code, which is the point of writing them first. The listener recorded a stamp and applied NOTHING - it had been a stub since it was written, with no changelog entry and no comment saying why - so a bottle added on the phone was invisible on the desktop until the desktop signed in again. It applies changes now, as they happen, and the device stops being a peer and becomes a view of the account. Three things it must not do, each learned today: not apply this device's own write coming back, not redraw over something being typed, and not fight the offline path. save_ takes a localOnly flag for exactly that: taking a change FROM the account and pushing it straight back is an echo, and with two devices listening it is an echo that bounces - and it must not move the local stamp, because nothing on this device changed, which is the fault that made local win against a newer account all day. Firebase's own offline support is still unused and worth a look another time. 2923 assertions.

## v1.8.14  ·  2026-09-06 03:28 PM ET

A half-taken update was leaving a phone with no tabs. BZ: when a mobile app loads a new version the tabs disappear, and a clear and re-open fixes it. The service worker's install swallowed a precache failure - a catch inside waitUntil - so the install SUCCEEDED with an empty or half-filled cache. Activate then deleted the old cache, claimed the page, and the reload landed on nothing. Clearing the site fixed it because that forces a fetch over the network instead of a cache-first miss. Failing the install is the correct outcome: the old worker stays in charge, the old cache stays intact, and the update is taken on the next visit when the network is willing. A version that will not download is better than one that half-downloads. The shell must be there or the install fails; the two data files are cached as a convenience, are network-first anyway, and can no longer hold an install back. Activate also checks the new cache can actually stand up - one lookup for index.html - before throwing the old one away, and the last-resort fallback looks in ANY cache rather than only the current one, because after a half-taken update the current cache is the empty one and looking only there returns undefined, which is a broken page rather than an old one. Verified by simulating it: a version update with the network dropping mid-precache, seven nav buttons before and seven after. 2901 assertions.

A key with no default was quietly handing the sync to the account. BZ's log: could not read bottleSaid from this device, taking the account copy instead - twice, followed by remote wins. bottleSaid is the store of what has been written about each bottle, and it shipped in 1.8.10 without an entry in S. load() compares a stored value against the SHAPE of S[k], so an undefined default makes every stored object look corrupt: the key was discarded on every single load, and a discarded key forces remote wins by design - correctly, since a device that lost a key must not push its emptiness over the account. The result was a device that could never win a sync and a toast saying some local data would not read, with no other sign anything was wrong. A missing default is not a cosmetic omission. It now has one, it follows the account because each sentence cost a lookup and describes the shelf rather than the device, and it MERGES rather than replaces so two devices that wrote about different bottles keep both. consistency.js has a fourteenth check: every stored key has a default in S. It found nothing else, and it would have caught this before it shipped. The other check in that pair caught the follow-on question in the same run - whether the key should travel at all - which is the pair working as intended. 1 new assertion, 2901 total.

What comes back is checked before it is shown, and the failure guarded against is not a wrong fact but a paragraph that says nothing or says it at length - under forty characters is refused, over nine hundred is cut. ## v1.6.92  ·  2026-09-05 11:51 AM ET

A price of 1,200 was becoming a bottle worth one dollar. parseFloat of the string 1,200 is 1 - not NaN, not 1200, ONE - and it passed validation because 1 is a perfectly good number. So a price typed or pasted with a thousands comma silently truncated to a dollar and nothing anywhere said so. Every spreadsheet writes thousands that way and both importers take their numbers from spreadsheets, which made this a live path to quietly wrong data rather than a curiosity. One reader now, used at all five places a number comes in from a person: it strips currency symbols, thousands separators and spaces, and refuses anything that is still not a number rather than guessing. 1,2 is refused, because it is not 12 and not 1, it is somebody's typo, and inventing a value from it is how a wrong price reaches a shared library. Found by feeding the engine the shapes a real import produces - a name of 500 characters, an emoji, a proof of 9999, an age spelled out in words, null everywhere - which is what somebody else's CSV looks like. Nothing threw on any of it, which is worth knowing, but not throwing is not the same as behaving sensibly and the comma was the one that was not. Also checked and clean: every one of the thirteen screens draws with an empty shelf and with exactly one bottle, which is the state every one of BZ's friends starts in. 14 new assertions, 2672 total.

## v1.6.69  ·  2026-09-05 12:24 AM ET

The account is the system of record, the masthead says where the shelf is being kept, and two keys were never syncing at all. BZ, watching the diagnostics: we need to make sure local is only used when online is not available, online is a clear preference. It was newest-wins on a timestamp, and newer is a claim each device makes about itself - a phone with a fast clock wins an argument it should lose, and one that has sat in a pocket can push a stale shelf over a good one the moment it wakes. The account wins now, and local wins only where it holds work the account has never seen: every successful push stamps pushedAt, so anything saved after it is unsent and anything before it is already up there. One existing assertion caught a mistake in the first attempt - an account with no stamp is an OLDER account rather than an empty one, and section 200 had asserted that since before the stamp existed. Two keys were wrong and one was invisible. libLedger was declared mergeable and never appeared in the sync list at all, so the ledger that stops the app paying twice for the same lookup was per device and a second device paid the whole bill again. tastingsSeen was missing too, which costs more than a lookup: a tasting applied on a phone would have been applied AGAIN on a desktop, logging the same pours twice. Both merge rather than replace, because a record of what has already happened must never shrink. The list lived in the render layer where the harness cannot see it, which is exactly why the fault sat there unnoticed; it has moved into L and a test now asserts that anything declared mergeable is actually synced. A wishlist that points outward, in the two ways BZ described: a short list sent by text, and a standing list a buddy can see. ## v1.6.66  ·  2026-09-04 10:34 PM ET

A tasting travels to the people who were there. When a flight is logged you pick who was in the room, and the evening reaches them: a bottle they already own is logged as a pour on their shelf without asking, because being there was the consent and they drank it, and a bottle they do not own is offered to their wishlist, their choice. What travels is the whole of what a photograph of the label was for - the bottle, its proof, its cask, where it was poured and on what night, and the note whoever poured it wrote, with that person's name still on it, because a note nobody wrote silently becoming somebody's own is how a shelf fills with opinions no one holds. The shape matters more than the feature. The host never writes to a guest: every uid-keyed node in this app is writable only by its owner, an admin may delete but never write, and that rule is worth more than the convenience of writing straight into somebody's shelf. So the host records the evening under their OWN account naming who was there, and each guest's device reads it on the next load and applies it with their own credentials, which is the same shape sharedWith already uses. Two things fall out for free: consent is attendance, because the record names the room, and a tasting cannot be forwarded, because a device not on that list has nothing to read. firebase-rules.json gains a tastings node, owner-written and readable by anybody the host shares a shelf with, and it must be pasted into the console. 20 new assertions, 2511 total. Untested with a second account, which is true of every sharing path in the app.

## v1.6.65  ·  2026-09-04 10:27 PM ET

The price eval, and the data it needed. An offer now gets two verdicts rather than one: fit and cost are independent questions, and blending them destroys the only thing worth knowing, because a bottle that suits the shelf at a poor price and one that suits nothing at a bargain both come out middling and call for opposite actions. The pair is the recommendation - buy it, want it and wait, cheap is not a reason. Allocation is urgency rather than a third axis: an allocated bottle at a fair price will not wait, the same bottle dear is a real decision, and a bottle the shelf does not want is still not wanted because it is rare, which is the line an app is most tempted to get wrong. Prices are kept when parsing now instead of thrown away, a struck-through sale reads as the lower figure, and free shipping over 99 dollars is no longer mistaken for a 99 dollar bottle. Three references in order: what YOU paid, then list price, then a figure the listing stated which is labeled a guess and never outranks the other two. Currency is never converted silently - the rate is typed in Settings with the date it was set, the app shows its working when it uses one, and with no rate it declines to compare rather than inventing one. Two faults surfaced while building it. paidFor returns an object and the verdict was handed it instead of the number, so the strongest comparison never fired at all. And paid had no way into the app but a CSV import, which is why 3 of 344 bottles carried one: the add form now asks what it cost, and a new Import what you paid takes a purchase spreadsheet, matches each line to a bottle already owned by word overlap rather than exact name, and fills in the price and date without ever adding a bottle. On BZ's own 39 receipts that is 27 confident matches and 10 more worth a look, and it took his shelf from 3 priced bottles to 21. 20 new assertions, 2491 total.

## v1.6.57  ·  2026-09-04 07:13 PM ET

Smoke runs to Octomore, ranges are scored as ranges, and the scoring is two rules instead of four. BZ asked whether smoke is really about coverage from none to heavy, from bourbon to Octomore. Then the better question, from buying a first Octomore without knowing where it sat: what happens when an edge point is on the shelf without the span in between. Under plain coverage nothing, and the shelf looks complete at the top - but bourbon and Octomore with nothing between is two ends and no ladder, and you cannot hear what peat does at any volume in between. Scoring the longest unbroken run was too blunt the other way, throwing the Octomore away as though it had not been bought. The rungs count and the holes between them discount, which is BZ's own suggestion and better than mine: that shelf reads 16 percent rather than 40 or 0. Holes above the highest rung held are not holes, they are the part of the range not reached, and coverage already counts those. Four mechanisms had piled onto one number - evenness, scarcity weights, a knee and tail ceiling, hole discounting - and they began contradicting each other, which is what two failing tests were actually reporting. Two remain and each fits in a sentence: a set is coverage times spread, a range is coverage discounted by its holes. The weights and the ceiling existed to keep 100 percent rare, and the extended ranges do that on their own. 2376 assertions.

Tapping it asks for a double wood or a triple wood or an unfinished single cask rather than a phrase from another axis. ## v1.6.44  ·  2026-09-04 01:48 PM ET

The barcode scanner works on an iPhone, and the situation tiles fit. BarcodeDetector is Chrome only, so the scanner had never worked on iOS at all - it opened the sheet and offered to let you type the number in, which is precisely the thing scanning exists to avoid, and some of the people about to test this are on iPhones. ZXing reads the same four formats where the native detector is missing, using its own browser reader rather than a hand-rolled canvas loop: it owns the canvas, the luminance conversion and the retry cadence, and a first attempt that rolled those by hand decoded nothing at all. Proved end to end by drawing a real EAN-13 from the published digit patterns and reading it back - written as 0012345678905 and returned as the 12-digit UPC-A it is, which upcKey resolves to the same pairing either way, so a bottle scanned on an iPhone and on a Pixel teaches the same barcode. The decoder is served from the app's own files rather than a CDN and is precached by the service worker, because scanning happens standing in a shop and a shop is where there is no signal to fetch a decoder with. It costs 330KB once, against a scan that fails at the only moment it matters. The reader is stopped alongside the camera stream, or the light stays on after the sheet closes. The four situations on Shop were laid out in three columns, so the fourth sat alone on a second row with two thirds of a row empty beside it; the grid fits itself now, four across on a wide screen and two by two on a phone, and a fifth will not need it touched again. 2290 assertions.

## v1.6.36  ·  2026-09-04 11:31 AM ET

A removed account cannot rebuild itself from the device that still has the shelf. Deleting an account was not enough on its own, and this was a real hole: the shelf lives on the person's device as well as in their account, and a first load that finds an empty account pushes the local copy up to seed it. That is right when somebody signs in on a new phone and exactly wrong the day after their account was removed - they would sign in and the shelf would rebuild itself from the browser that still had it, undoing the deletion without anybody noticing. A removal now leaves a timestamp at wiped, written in the SAME update as the wipe so a wipe can never land without the thing that stops it being undone. On sign-in that timestamp is read BEFORE anything can decide to push, and a device whose shelf has not been touched since then clears itself: bottles, flights, the pour log, edits, corrections, favorites, barcodes and both ledgers, because all of that is shelf and a fresh account should not be haunted by the old one. Settings and the device log stay, the log because it is the only thing that can explain the reset afterward. Anything built AFTER the wipe is the person genuinely starting again and syncs as normal, which is what makes this a reset rather than a ban. The tombstone is not writable by the account except when it deletes itself, or a device could erase the record of its own deletion and push the old shelf straight back. 11 new assertions, 2222 total. firebase-rules.json changed and must be pasted into the console.
