An admin can remove another account, and the rules let them delete it without letting them change it. Deleting your own account shipped last build; an admin could not remove anybody else's, because every uid-keyed node was writable only by its owner. Each one now reads own, or not-exists and admin - so an admin may clear a shelf and may not alter one, which is the whole distinction: running the library should not come with the power to edit other people's bottles. People, as an admin appears on the Buddies screen, which is the only screen that lists people, as a separate card below the ordinary ones and never mixed into the row you press to share a shelf with a friend. It clears the same nine places a self-delete does, by the same accountWipe, so the two cannot drift apart. Type REMOVE to confirm. What it cannot do is remove the sign-in: Firebase's client SDK can only delete the account it is signed in as, so another person's login is a console job under Authentication, and this says so plainly rather than leaving an admin believing an account is gone while the password still works. A refusal names the likely cause, which is a console holding older rules than the file. 2211 assertions. firebase-rules.json changed and must be pasted into the console.

Two things stay and both are asserted so a later change cannot quietly start taking them: anything published to the shared library belongs to the library once it is there and removing it would take it off everybody else, and admins is write:false by design so an account can no more demote itself than promote itself - that line is a console job. The data and the login are two steps and can fail separately, so it reports which happened rather than claiming the whole thing worked; a sign-in that needs a fresh login says so instead of leaving a deleted shelf behind a working password. Every cell opens what is in it. The sharpest line is its own: bought more than once and never poured once, which is money on a shelf doing nothing and the only finding here that can be acted on tonight. ## v1.6.23  ·  2026-09-04 08:21 AM ET

Case is tidied only where none was given, and a live bug that rewrote real names on save is gone. The question was whether names could always be stored title case and notes sentence case. The shelf answered it: of 325 names and every distillery on it, not one is in all capitals and not one is in all lower case, and only 9 of 930 tasting notes begin with a small letter, so there was nothing to migrate. Running title case over what is there would have damaged 37 of the 325 - A'Bunadh to A'bunadh, E.H. Taylor to E.h. Taylor, PX Sherry to Px Sherry, OBSV to Obsv, Feis Ile to Feis ILe, Bottled-In-Bond to Bottled-in-bond, and every Finished in Port to Finished In Port. A rule that wrong on a ninth of the shelf is not a rule. Worse, a version of it was already running: typedName put every save through titleCase, which is the shelf LABEL caser and lower-cases small words wherever they fall and expands its own abbreviations, so opening any of 27 bottles and pressing Save changes corrupted the name - Ardbeg Anthology the Harpy's Tale, Glenmorangie the Lasanta, Triple Cask STR. The test now is whether the text carries case at all. Both capitals and small letters means somebody meant it and it is returned untouched; ALL CAPS or all lower means nothing was said, and only then is anything decided - a one-letter prefix takes its capital where a possessive does not, an initialism written with stops is restored, Mc takes its second capital, a hyphen starts a word inside one, and a small word stays small in the middle but never at either end. Notes follow the same test with sentence case, deciding only the sentence starts and leaving every other word as it came. Measured against the real shelf: 0 of 325 names touched. Distillery and finish are tidied on the same rule, at both places a form is read. 2137 assertions.

## v1.5.11  ·  2026-09-03 08:55 PM ET

Two tabs of the app stop fighting each other. The storage listener adopted another tab's change and called appLog to say so; appLog writes the log straight into localStorage; the other tab read that as a change worth adopting, logged it, wrote it, and back it came. With two tabs open it never stopped: hundreds of the same line, and every round redrew every screen, so on a PC nothing could be clicked because the DOM was being rebuilt under the pointer, and on a phone the nav went with it. The log is now ignored by the listener, because it is a tab's own diary and the one key whose adoption writes another key. The redraw is coalesced too: a save writes all eighteen keys, so a single change in one tab arrived in the other as eighteen storage events and eighteen full redraws. ## v1.5.9  ·  2026-09-03 08:32 PM ET

Publishing to the library actually publishes, and the green box on Time for a Taste holds its height. The code was never duplicated, only the history, and this entry replaces it. Publishing: the updates are keyed by PATH, and running them through fbEncode escaped the slashes, so a publish wrote one child called catalog~fproducts~fweller_12 at the top of the shared node, reported success, and the library received nothing, which is why the count never cleared. One rule now, fbEncodePaths, at all four call sites: the batch publish, the library rename, publishing after a purchase, and the undo. ## v1.5.3  ·  2026-09-03 07:49 PM ET

Three structural changes from the review, none of them a bug fix. Three predicates, and every shape that walks bottles reads them; the iteration shapes still differ on purpose, because asking about one whisky should stop at the first hit and asking about all of them should make one pass. Nine scenarios, each written as the thing that actually went wrong. And the render half, 8900 lines with no assertions against it, now has render.js: not a hand-written expectation, which is only a third copy of the rule, but the same fact read off every screen that draws it and compared to the engine and to the other screens. ## v1.5.2  ·  2026-09-03 07:32 PM ET

Fortification, from the second review pass. A key that would not read on the way in no longer wipes the account: a discarded key leaves the empty initial value in its place, and that emptiness was still treated as this device's copy, so a newer local stamp pushed an empty shelf over a good one. A device that lost anything now hands the decision to the account and says so. Everything is backed up to one file, not just the twelve CSV columns: the history, the 36 designed flights, the edits, favorites, barcodes, wishlist and dismissals had no copy BZ could hold. Restore reads the file, refuses one that is not ours by name rather than half applying it, leaves alone any key the file does not carry, and says what it is about to replace before it does. Two tabs stop overwriting each other: each held its own state and neither saw the other's writes. A full disk says so on every failed save rather than once an hour ago. Junk in a number field becomes nothing rather than NaN, which compares false against itself and traveled to the shared library. Offline is reported as offline rather than as a lookup that failed twenty seconds later. The X that deletes a log row is 44 tall to the thumb while still looking like a 26px mark, grown down and right so it cannot steal a tap from the control beside it. Removing an entry from the shared library can be undone, which is the only destructive action that changes what everybody sees and was the one with a confirm and no undo. And Escape is handled once instead of by two listeners whose order decided which ran.

Everything on the open list, in one build. ## v1.26.37  ·  2026-09-03 04:05 PM ET

The shelf counts what you own once per redraw instead of once per whisky. ownedCount walks every bottle to answer for one whisky, and the shelf asked it once per product: 325 products against 344 bottles is 111,800 comparisons for a single redraw, then again for the type tiles, the totals and the shelf value. One pass over the bottles into a key to count map is 344, and the map is built once by renderShelf and handed to everything below it. shelfFilter takes the map when it is given one and builds its own when it is not, so a caller that forgets is slower and never wrong. The count is the same count, a bottle marked gone is not owned, and the harness asserts the map against ownedCount for every product rather than trusting that two ways of counting agree. Typing in the shelf search settles for 150ms before redrawing: a six-letter search was six whole redraws. The filter chips still redraw at once, because a tap is a decision and a letter is not.

An admin can rename an entry in the shared library. The editor had fields for proof, distillery, age, cask, region and price and not for the name, which is the field it is usually opened for: an entry published in whatever case it happened to be typed. A rename there is a move, not a write, because the library is keyed by the name, so the entry is written at the new key and cleared from the old one in the same update or everybody reads the bottle twice under two spellings. ## v1.26.34  ·  2026-09-03 03:43 PM ET

An empty list on this device and nothing on the account are the same state. Firebase cannot store an empty list or an empty map: write one and the key is simply absent afterward, so an empty wishlist never matched an account holding nothing and the same 179 bytes went up on every load, for ever. Same shape as the key-order fault, three orders of magnitude smaller. Emptying a list the account does hold is still a real change and still goes up.

## v1.26.33  ·  2026-09-03 03:36 PM ET

Comparing what is on the account no longer depends on key order. The merge keys are built with Object.assign of local into remote, which gives the same content in a different order, and JSON.stringify is order sensitive, so edits, wish, dismissals and proposals compared unequal to the copy just read and pushed 61KB on every load with nothing in them changed. All three sides of the comparison now share one signature: object keys sorted, arrays left alone because their order is the data for bottles and history.

## v1.26.32  ·  2026-09-03 03:24 PM ET

A load hands over whatever the account is missing, as soon as it completes. fbFirstLoad calls save_ to persist the merge, and save_ guards its push behind FB.loaded, which is set four lines later: a load scheduled no push at all. Nothing reached the account until the next change, and that push carried the whole shelf because nothing had been recorded as sent yet, so it timed out at 250KB and the account stamp sat unchanged through an hour of work. The push now runs at the end of the load, seeded from what it just read, so it carries the difference. A device already in step with the account sends nothing at all.

## v1.26.31  ·  2026-09-03 03:16 PM ET

A null inside a list is dropped on the way to the database, not just an undefined. The bad run record is already saved: an undefined in an array becomes null the moment localStorage round-trips it, and Firebase reads a null inside a list as a delete of that index, so the run would have come back a pour short even once the record stopped being written wrong. A null FIELD still stands, because several records use one to mean known to be nothing.

## v1.26.30  ·  2026-09-03 03:14 PM ET

Pushes stop being refused by the database. With the SDK finally reporting its errors, two came back and both are hard write failures. Firebase will not accept a key holding a period, hash, dollar, slash or bracket, and this app keys edits, custom, deleted, favorites and dismissals by the product name: 18 of the 325 shipped keys hold one, Elmer T. Lee among them, so any push carrying one was refused whole. That is the root of the week. The account stayed behind, and a later load handed the older copy back, which is how a bought bottle and a flight pour disappeared while the library kept the whisky. Keys are escaped on the way up and restored on the way down, escape character first so a name holding a tilde survives. The second refusal was an undefined at history pours 5: a wish pour has no key, and mapping a cast straight to it wrote undefined into the record of a run. That map now falls back to the name, and undefined is stripped from every payload on the way out.

## v1.26.28  ·  2026-09-03 02:45 PM ET

A push carries the difference instead of the whole shelf. Diagnostics caught the write for the bottle purchase never coming back inside twenty seconds, which is the other half of the loss fixed two versions ago: the load no longer overwrites newer local work, but the write still has to land. fbPush sent every key on every save with set, which on this shelf is 31KB of bottles and 161KB of custom flights, re-uploaded to log a pour or star a favorite. It now sends only the keys whose value differs from what was last written, using update rather than set so the children it does not name are left alone. The record of what was pushed is written after the write lands, not when it is sent, or a failed push would never be retried. Saves within a second of each other are coalesced into one push, and Diagnostics logs the size and the keys of every push, successful or not.

A first load can no longer replace newer local work. ## v1.26.20  ·  2026-09-03 12:56 PM ET

Press publish on 49 and you still have 49, and it is the change made two versions ago that caused it. libraryEntry was taught not to publish a flight-card note — correctly, since a note written onto a flight card is a prompt for a room rather than a description of a whisky, and the published entry does not carry tnFrom so nobody downstream could tell. correctionFor was not taught the same thing. It still counted the absence of that note from the library as a gap, so those bottles queued for publishing, published nothing, and queued again on the next render. The button worked, the write went out, and the count could not move. A note the publisher will never send is not a difference. correctionFor asks whether the note is a prompt before counting it, and the test states the pairing directly rather than checking either side alone: publish the entry, then ask whether it is still pending, and the answer has to be no. It also asserts the other direction, since a rule that queues nothing is as broken as one that queues for ever — a proof the library lacks is still a correction, and publishing it settles it. Sixth instance this week of two functions holding one rule with only one of them taught, and the last untested pair in the publish path. +6 tests.

## v1.26.19  ·  2026-09-03 12:48 PM ET

The library read 0 whiskies straight after publishing to it, and offered to publish the entire shelf again. publishBatch sets LIB.products to null on the way out, deliberately, so the next read comes from Firebase rather than from a copy that is now wrong — and then it called draw, which renders whatever LIB.products holds. Null holds nothing, so the screen said 0 whiskies, and pendingForLibrary compares the shelf against that same empty object and found every bottle missing, which is where Publish 328 came from. Going Home and back fixed it because that path goes through the fetch. The fetch is its own function now, load, which gets the library if it is not held and then draws; draw only ever renders what is already there. Everything that clears the cache calls load, and nothing calls draw across a change. Editing an entry is the one caller that legitimately still calls draw, because it updates the held copy in place rather than clearing it. Also, and separately, the numbers on that screen were never wrong — the library really did hold 329, and both the 0 and the 328 were this same stale render.

A note written onto a flight card has been going into the shared library as though it were a tasting note. libraryEntry published anything carrying a nose and never looked at tnFrom, and the entry it writes does not keep tnFrom — so once a prompt is in the library nothing downstream can tell it from a description, and it reads as authoritative to everybody. Every other path in the app had already been taught that a prompt is not a note: the fill-in queue, the diff, the bottle screen, the publish review. It stays behind now, the rest of the entry still goes, and a real note carries its tnSrc so the library can say where a note came from rather than imply it. Take back N invented notes appears when there are any, names the count, and clears only the note — every other field of the entry stays. ## v1.26.13  ·  2026-09-03 09:09 AM ET

The run was written locally, never reached the account, and was then deleted by the account. Two faults, and either one alone would have been survivable. First, FB.saving. It exists to keep two pushes from racing, and it is released only inside the promise that took it — so a set that never came back, which is what a dropped connection looks like, left it true and the guard at the top of fbPush returned early for every push after that, silently, for the rest of the session. A lock that can only be released by the thing holding it is not a lock, it is a single point of failure. It times out now, says so in the log, and the next push runs. Second, the load. fbFirstLoad did S[k] = remote[k] for every synced key, and edits is a MAP keyed by product carrying the 161 tasting notes the run had just written. The account had not received them, so the next load replaced them with nothing and the bottles went back to their flight prompts and the queue back to 201. Dismissals, favorites and barcodes were already merging for exactly this reason and edits was not. Keyed maps merge — remote wins where both sides hold the key, and a key only one side has survives — and lists are still taken whole, because a shelf has an order and a length and two of them interleaved is not a shelf. The rule is a function now rather than an inline branch, and the test drives the real loss end to end: the run's note, an account that never saw it, a load, the merge, the catalog rebuild, and the queue. 161 bottles were filled in and the queue still said 201, because null is not a value Firebase will keep. enhanceDiff cleared the flight-prompt marker by writing tnFrom null into edits. edits is in SYNC_KEYS, and Firebase DELETES any key whose value is null rather than storing it — so the tombstone was stripped on the way up, came back absent, and mergeCatalog's Object.assign let the base's own tnFrom through again on the next load. The marker was immortal, every filled bottle stayed in the queue, and a second run would have paid for 201 lookups to learn nothing. Second defense, because the first alone leaves the bottles already filled stranded: tnSrc records where a note came from, absent for one we wrote onto a flight card and model or review for a real one, so a bottle carrying a real note outranks a stale marker whatever became of the marker. An overwrite is the opposite — something is already there, somebody may have meant it, and it is about to be replaced. The batch splits on that line. ## v0.1.55  ·  2026-09-01 09:23 AM ET

On the current sheet that made 169 rows out of 39 actual decisions, asking the same question 4.3 times per bottle. buildDecisions writes a Decisions tab with one row per bottle-and-match: your name, their record, the source, the score and what accepting it would affect, with a verdict column to mark Y or N. Rows that would bring tasting notes sort to the top, since those carry the most and a run of easy rejections at the head of a list wastes the attention. ## v0.1.54  ·  2026-09-01 09:12 AM ET

Flight re-instantiation, the last backlog item buildable without Firebase or an API key. A flight is a template and its six bottles are one cast of it, so Sherry Is Not One Thing can be run again next year with different pours and still be the same flight — the same question asked of a different shelf. A flight that has been run gains Run it again, which reads the variable back out of its own tag, looks at every bottle the flight has ever used, and proposes a cast from what is open now with the previously poured pushed toward the back. Repeating one pour is fine, sometimes right for a control; repeating all six is just running it again. One thing the first version got wrong and the real data caught: holding the variable still is not enough. Sherry Is Not One Thing is all Scotch and no smoke, and re-casting on the cask alone produced five Irish Spots — a fine flight and a different one. Where the existing cast is uniform in a field, that uniformity is part of the design and now travels with it, so the re-cast comes back six Laphroaigs, four of them at a matched 96 proof with the wood as the only variable. Keeping a cast makes a new flight rather than overwriting the original, because run one is a record of what was actually poured that night and should not be edited by a later idea. +24 tests.

Angel's Envy Port Wine Barrel Finish scored zero against Angel's Envy Bourbon Port Cask Finish, which is very probably the same whiskey. Both of those pairs now surface at 0.75, in the band that gets reviewed by hand, rather than vanishing. enrichRetryMisses reads the MISS names out of the existing sheet, removes those rows so a retry does not sit beside its own failure, takes those bottles off the done list and hands back to the normal batching — so a matching fix can be tested against the third of the shelf it affects instead of re-running all 325.

## v0.1.48  ·  2026-09-01 08:36 AM ET

The enrichment run is roughly three times faster, from about 35 minutes to 12. Three things were costing the time and one of them was daft: the trigger fired every five minutes for a four-minute batch, so a minute in eight was spent waiting for the clock. It now fires every minute, with a script lock so a tick arriving mid-batch is a no-op rather than a second run doubling every lookup and racing on the saved progress. The 700 millisecond pause between every call was over-cautious — Pour Picks allows 60 requests a minute and we alternate two independent services, so each was seeing under half its allowance. 350ms holds under one call per second per source. And the second search query is now skipped once the first has returned enough results to score against, which for most bottles takes the work from five calls to three. Batch size goes to 70 to use the six-minute window properly. Nothing about the matching, the gating or the buckets changed — this is the same run, done sooner.

## v0.1.46  ·  2026-09-01 08:29 AM ET

The full-shelf run came back and its headline finding was a bug in my own matcher. 69 of the 84 conflicts were about proof, every one at a perfect 1.00 name match, and every one was the source being wrong rather than the shelf: Maker's Mark 101 returned as 90 proof, Sazerac 100 Proof as 90, Kilchoman 100 percent Islay as 116, Belle Meade Reserve 108.3 as 90. Four of those bottles state their proof in their own name and ours matched the label every time. The cause was the change made two versions ago to score names against the shorter of the two, which fixed one failure and created another: it made any source name that is a PREFIX of ours a perfect match, so Maker's Mark matched Maker's Mark 101 and Ardbeg 10 Years Old matched Ardbeg 10 Years Cask Strength. A gate now runs before the score — any number or expression word present in one name and absent from the other means a different bottling, whatever the remaining words agree on — and it clears all eleven test pairs including every real failure from the run. Proof is also demoted from CONFLICT to INFO permanently, because the gate is not sufficient on its own: Blue Spot matched the correct record and still came back 92 for a 117-proof whiskey. Our proofs came off labels and off Ohio state retail and are the better source, so a disagreement is now something to glance at rather than something to apply.

## v0.1.45  ·  2026-09-01 01:05 AM ET

enrichOvernight added, so the shelf finishes itself. Apps Script kills a single execution at six minutes, which is why the full run works in batches — but it can also schedule itself, and it should have from the start rather than asking BZ to click Run eight times at one in the morning. enrichOvernight installs a trigger that fires every five minutes, runs a batch, and removes its own trigger the moment the shelf is done. It does the first batch immediately rather than waiting five minutes, it refuses to stack triggers because a second one would double every lookup, and stopOvernight ends it early while keeping the progress. It shares the same saved progress list the manual runs use, so it picks up wherever the clicking stopped and running enrichFull by hand afterward is harmless. runEnrich_ now returns how many bottles are left rather than a sheet URL, which is what lets a scheduled run know when to stop, and its log line no longer prints 40.0 for 40. The ceiling test after the field fixes: 13 of 14 found, 7 with notes — and the split is clean, WHISKY:EDITION hitting 7 of 14 with notes on every hit, Pour Picks hitting 12 of 14 with none, exactly as its record shape predicted.

## v0.1.44  ·  2026-09-01 12:56 AM ET

Both sources fixed against their real response shapes, taken from a raw dump rather than from a README. The ceiling test found 13 of 14 widely stocked bottles and not a single tasting note, which was a field-name bug in both handlers rather than a coverage verdict. WHISKY:EDITION wraps its detail response as ok, lang, item — the record is one level down, and the code was reading tasting_notes off the top level, getting null every time. Pour Picks needed no detail call at all: get_bottle came back empty while the search record already carried everything, and its real fields are id, name, distillery, category, region, proof, age_years, price_usd, flavors, pairings, profile, description, popularity_tier and data_last_updated. The important thing that dump settled is that Pour Picks has no nose, palate or finish. It has flavor tags, a body-sweetness-char profile scored one to five, and a one-line description. Those are genuinely useful and none of them is a tasting note, so they are reported in their own INFO bucket and can never be written into a notes column. One real prize in there: popularity_tier is a measure of how widely known a bottle is, which is exactly the correction the obscurity field has needed since it was seeded from distillery footprint and never checked on any of the 325 — a disagreement now surfaces as a CONFLICT with the tier shown. Report rows are also sorted by bucket before they are written, since in bottle order a CONFLICT would sit somewhere among 325 rows and never be found, and each batch logs its own tally.

## v0.1.43  ·  2026-09-01 12:50 AM ET

A ceiling test added, because the probe was measuring the wrong thing. It sampled only bottles with no notes, alphabetically, which handed it three Barrell Craft Spirits and three Angel's Envy single barrels — the hardest corner of the shelf and no evidence about whether the sources work at all. The bottles without notes are also skewed by construction: the flight cards covered the whiskies worth putting in a flight, which are the recognisable ones, so what is left is 20 percent obscure against 12 percent for the rest. enrichCeiling picks the opposite: known, standard release, no single barrels, one per distillery so it cannot cluster, and cheapest first, since cheap and standard is the best available proxy for widely stocked. That yields Buffalo Trace, Rittenhouse, Jim Beam Black, Redbreast 12, Lagavulin 8 and nine more. If these are found, the sources work and the only question is whether they reach the 138; if these miss, the sources are thin and the idea stops. It also reports how many hits actually carried tasting notes, which is the thing being bought — a hit that confirms a proof we already have is not worth a full run. The diagnostics from the last probe did their job: they showed Pour Picks returning 29 Aberlours and 21 Ardbegs, so the endpoint and the query rewrite are working, and every rejection in that run was correct — Gold Spot is not Green Spot, Aberlour Double Charred is not Double Cask Matured, Baker's High Rye is not Baker's Single Barrel 7 Year.

## v0.1.42  ·  2026-09-01 12:45 AM ET

The enrichment probe was returning one hit in twenty, and both causes were mine. First: the Pour Picks endpoint answers in Server-Sent Events, not JSON — event: message followed by a data: line — and the code called JSON.parse on the whole body, so every reply from a perfectly healthy endpoint was thrown away unread. Responses are now parsed as either plain JSON or SSE, taking the last data frame that parses. Second, and larger: the search query was the entire label. Aberlour Double Cask Matured Batch AB16 3-21 16 Year Old Single Malt Scotch Whisky is fourteen words and no full-text search will ever match it; the single bottle that did hit, Ardbeg An Oa, is three words, which was the pattern rather than a coincidence. Searching now works the way a person does: query the distillery, which all 325 bottles have and which is one to three words, ask for 25 results, and score every one against the full name — high recall, with the two-threshold matcher supplying the precision. A second query on the first distinctive words catches anything filed under a brand rather than a distillery, and the search stops early once a sure match is found. Pour Picks also gets its real category enum, taken from the tool schema rather than guessed, with fallbacks to scotch_blend and then to no category at all, so a wrong category never reads as an absent bottle. Every miss now records what was searched, how many results came back and the best name among them, so the next failure explains itself instead of costing another round trip.

## v0.1.41  ·  2026-09-01 12:30 AM ET

The full run batches forty bottles at a time to stay inside the six-minute limit and saves progress, so it is run repeatedly until it reports DONE. ## v0.1.40  ·  2026-09-01 12:23 AM ET

enrich.py written so the shelf reconciliation is one command when the sources can be reached. It cannot be run from here — there is no background execution, and the container's egress allowlist returns 403 for both thewhiskyedition.com and the Pour Picks endpoint — so this is the tool rather than the result. It resolves each bottle through the source chain, WHISKY:EDITION then Pour Picks, and sorts every finding into CONFLICT, WEAK, FILL, SUPERSEDE, CONFIRM or MISS. It never writes data.json; it writes a report you read, and --apply takes only the rows you left in it. Two decisions worth recording. Name matching is scored against the shorter name rather than the longer, because our names carry everything off the label while a source may hold three words — measuring against the longer scored Booker's Beam House at 0.25 against its own short form and would have thrown away a correct match. And there are two thresholds rather than one, because no single cutoff separates Green Spot Montelena from Green Spot Leoville Barton: they share a two-word brand, nothing else, and score identically. Anything between the thresholds is reported as WEAK with the matched source name beside it, is never applied automatically, and has to be promoted by hand. The tool does not pretend to know. Verified end to end against fixtures with the network stubbed: buckets sort correctly, the report writes, apply takes FILL and leaves WEAK alone.

## v0.1.34  ·  2026-08-31 11:39 PM ET

The nav bar can no longer cover content, using MadGolf's structure rather than more padding. Padding was never going to fix it: with the whole page scrolling, a fixed bar overlays whatever is at the bottom of the viewport at EVERY scroll position, and bottom padding only guarantees you can scroll far enough to reach the last element. MadGolf makes the body a fixed-height flex column at 100dvh with overflow hidden, scrolls the active screen inside it, and puts the nav last in that column, so content physically cannot pass under the bar. That is now the structure here. Three consequences handled: the footer was appended to the body and would have landed below the nav, so it lives inside the home screen; the nav needed position relative or its z-index would have been ignored, dropping it behind the modal overlay when rule 19 puts it above on purpose; and the Shop screen no longer computes its own height because the screen it sits in already has one. The sticky filter bars now stick to the scrolling screen rather than the window, which is what they were always meant to do. An audit check fails the build if the body stops being a flex column, the screen stops scrolling, or a fixed nav comes back.

ship.py now tells you which files to redeploy instead of listing everything every time. ## v0.1.30  ·  2026-08-31 11:18 PM ET

Build a flight is now an AI-assisted design, and Shop holds its answer on screen. Give the builder a variable and a premise and it sends your OPEN shelf and the house rules to a design service, which comes back with a titled flight, a note on what each pour is doing, three or four paragraphs on why it is built that way, and one bottle to buy that would improve it. The architecture is the point: the model proposes, local code verifies, and nothing reaches the screen unchecked. Every returned name must resolve to a bottle that is actually open on your shelf or it is dropped and shown in a Dropped list; a sealed bottle is rejected as not open; a repeat is rejected as listed twice; and a proposal that leaves fewer than three real pours fails outright rather than serving a short flight. Two holes in that verification found by driving invented names through it: a fuzzy fallback accepted Completely Invented Whisky 12 because it shared the token 12 with something real, and Bourbon 12 reduced to the single token 12 and matched anything containing it. A strict match threshold and a two-token floor now reject all seven invented names in the test set while still resolving real ones however they are worded. Without the service configured the builder falls back to the local recommender, and the whole thing still works offline. Shop was rebuilt as a column: the search sits at the top, what you already own scrolls in the middle, and the verdict holds the bottom of the page so the answer is never below the fold. Bottle lookup moved into Shop, where you are standing in front of the bottle, and the verdict re-judges as you correct a field. One process failure worth recording: a slice boundary matched a marker earlier in the file than intended and duplicated 2,425 lines; the file was checked function by function, the duplicate excised, and every definition confirmed to appear exactly once before anything else was done. +32 tests.

Logo recoloured, type filter compacted, and the layout adapts to a desktop. The mark was black line art on a solid white square, so it read as a white box against the parchment page; it is now the app's own ink on a transparent ground, with the anti-aliased edges preserved by using luminance as the alpha rather than a hard cut. The home-screen icons keep a parchment ground on purpose, because iOS puts them on a wallpaper where a transparent mark would disappear. An audit check now fails the build if the mark ever loses its alpha channel, verified against a deliberately flattened copy. The twelve type chips wrapped two and three rows deep and pushed the shelf itself off screen; they are now one pick list that opens over the list rather than displacing it, shows a tick and a count per row, reads All types or the single name or the number chosen when shut, and closes on any tap outside it. On a wide screen the single phone column was stretching to the full window, which made every row a long thin ribbon and the summary tiles enormous: the measure is now capped, the tiles run six across, the summary charts sit two across so the dashboard fits one screen instead of six, and the nav bar is centered rather than spanning the whole width.

## v0.1.26  ·  2026-08-31 10:42 PM ET

Screen actions moved to the top on MadGolf's pattern, and the shelf gained an importer. Every list screen now carries its actions in the header beside the title — Add bottle and Import on the shelf, Build a flight on Flights, Spin and Clear holds on Time for a Taste — and both detail screens put their buttons in a row directly under Back, so the thing you came to do is not below the record you came to read. Import takes a CSV from Only Drams, a spreadsheet of your own, or an export from this app: column names are matched loosely across about fifty aliases, so Name or Bottle or Product all read as the name and ABV or Proof or Strength all read as the strength. Only Drams exports ABV rather than proof, so anything under 60 in a strength column is read as an ABV and doubled — 43 becomes 86, 57.5 becomes 115. Categories match loose spellings and a region names its country, so Islay reads as Scotch and Single Pot Still as Irish; an unrecognised category falls back to bourbon and says so on the row rather than filing it silently. Nothing is written until you have seen the preview, which counts what would be added, what is already on the shelf and would gain a sealed spare, what repeats inside the file, and what has no usable proof, and lists the rows worth a second look with their line numbers. A downloadable template and a column specification sit in the same dialog, and the template round-trips through its own importer with no issues, which is the only test that makes it a template. One real bug found on the way: a row repeated inside the file was being overwritten as already-owned, so re-importing an export with a duplicate line would have added two sealed spares for one bottle. +51 tests.

## v0.1.25  ·  2026-08-31 10:37 PM ET

Data QA became a gate, and every US distillery is now on the map. validate.py checks the catalog against itself and against the rules of the categories — a bottled-in-bond that is not 100 proof, a name stating 12 years against a stored 10, a Scotch below 80 proof, a region on something that is not Scotch, a wine verdict with no finish, a duplicate name, a bottle pointing at a product that is not there, and the stocking rule of exactly one open per whisky — and it runs inside ship.py, which now refuses to ship on any data error. It found a real 44-bottle gap on the first run: a known finish with no wine verdict, so Ardbeg Uigeadail and forty-three others were neither wine nor wood. All are classified now, taking wine-cask to 97 and confirmed wood-only from 7 to 13, and no finish is left unclassified. Warnings fell from 50 to 6, of which four are correct filings the checker flags conservatively and two are secondary prices worth your eye. On the map, all 53 US distilleries carry real coordinates and draw as pins over the state shading — Kentucky and Tennessee at the same granularity as Islay, so Bardstown's seven distilleries and Louisville's six read as the clusters they are. Zoom ceilings are now per layer because one number cannot resolve a map 4.5 degrees wide and one 35.6 wide: Scotland 80x for the Islay trio, the United States 900x for Bardstown, and both verified to zero overlapping pins at their ceiling. Brands sharing a site fan onto a small ring so neither pin is unreachable, deterministically and never more than a mile from the truth. +45 tests.

## v0.1.24  ·  2026-08-31 10:30 PM ET

Tasting-note provenance corrected, and history is now visible. I wrote the flight cards, color and nose and palate and finish included, so the 187 notes lifted off them are prompts written to guide a taster — not the distiller's notes, and not sourced. They now say so wherever they appear rather than posing as fact, and a test forbids any card note from claiming a producer as its origin. Notes carry one of three origins: written for a card, the producer's own, or your own tasting, and you can now enter or correct all four columns yourself on any bottle, saved as yours. The Info panel reports how much of the shelf is described and how much of that is only prompts. lookup.gs ships alongside the app: an Apps Script backend holding your API key server-side that serves the Add a bottle lookup and, run from the editor, batch-sources tasting notes for the bottles that have none into a CSV on your Drive for review before anything is merged — the app copies the missing names for you. Time for a Taste gained a history below the machine, tabbed between Pours and Flights, each with a six-month bar of activity, a tappable log back to the bottle or flight, and a way to remove a mistaken entry. One real bug found by a precomputed expectation: the log stamped its row index after filtering by kind, so removing an entry would have deleted a different one from the history. +27 tests.

## v0.1.23  ·  2026-08-31 10:25 PM ET

Six changes from on-device use. Shop moved next to Shelf in the tab bar, and Taste and Flights now carry line-art Glencairns — one glass and three — instead of geometric glyphs. Completion is confirmed and dated rather than assumed: tapping a glass on Time for a Taste opens the bottle with its notes and an explicit I poured this, and a flight is marked run through a dialog that asks when, defaulting to today but accepting any past date, because a flight gets written up the morning after as often as the night of. The date is validated properly — the 31st of February and a future date are both refused rather than silently stored. Flights gained the same browsing the shelf has: search across title, variable, premise and the names of the pours; filters for not run, run and fully pourable, plus type chips counting flights rather than bottles; and sorts for curriculum order, name, most ready, lowest proof and recently run, with a never-run flight sorting last rather than posing as the oldest. Pours on the proof ladder are now tappable and open that bottle. And the notes you asked for turned out to already exist: the flight cards carry color, nose, palate and finish for all 221 core pours, so those are lifted onto the catalog and 187 bottles now show the distiller's notes in sheet order on their own panel, credited to the card they came from. +41 tests.

## v0.1.22  ·  2026-08-31 10:19 PM ET

The shelf gained sorting and six more filters. Sort by name, proof either direction, distillery, oldest, dearest or cheapest — and a missing value sorts last on every key rather than reading as zero, so a no-age-stated bottle never poses as the youngest and an unpriced one never leads the cheapest list. Ties break on name so the order never depends on insertion. A More filters panel opens under the existing search, status and type rows and adds proof band, recognition, release, occasion, Scotch region, cask and age, every chip carrying how many bottles it would show. Cask and age are one-of-three rather than multi-select because wine cask, wood only and no finish are mutually exclusive states — and a bottle whose wine status is unknown counts as neither, rather than being swept into wood only. All facets AND together, the button shows how many are on, and there is a Clear all. The empty state now says how many filters produced it instead of a generic line. +36 tests.

## v0.1.21  ·  2026-08-31 10:16 PM ET

Time for a Taste can no longer land on a dead spin. The reels used to roll blind and could stop on a combination nothing on the shelf satisfies, leaving three empty glasses; a spin now picks a pourable bottle that satisfies whatever reels are held and describes it with the ones that are not, each unheld reel either narrowing to that bottle or opening up, so at least one pour always stands. Holds that rule out everything return nothing at all rather than spinning, and the machine names which holds are impossible so you know which to release. Verified by driving 400 spins over the real 325-bottle shelf and 400 more over the fixture: no empty paylines, holds always honored, and 94 percent of spins fill all three glasses. Type on the machine was too small to read at arm's length: reel labels, faces, the hold marker, the Spin button and the glass captions all went up, the reel value area is taller so two-word faces like American Malt sit on two lines rather than clipping, and the Glencairns are larger. +9 tests.

## v0.1.20  ·  2026-08-31 10:14 PM ET

Map pins were unusable: sized in pixels while the SVG works in degrees, so a single-bottle pin drew at 64 percent of Scotland's width and the dots covered the country. Pin radius is now a fraction of the map's own span, scaled between a floor and a ceiling against the largest count in that layer, so one bottle reads at 4.3 percent of the width and twelve at 9 percent, and the same function works on a map 4.5 degrees wide and one 203 degrees wide. Label and count type sizes are map units for the same reason. A second, deeper fault fixed alongside it: pins shrank by the square root of the zoom while the view shrinks by the zoom itself, so pins grew on screen as you zoomed in and a cluster could never come apart however far you pushed. They now hold a constant screen size. The zoom ceiling went from 40x to 80x because Ardbeg, Lagavulin and Laphroaig only separate above about 31x, and a ceiling of 40 left no room past the one view the map exists for. Overlapping pin pairs across Scotland fall from 30 at full extent to none at maximum zoom. Ten new tests pin the geometry, including that no pin exceeds a tenth of the map on the real data and that the Islay trio overlaps when zoomed out and separates at the ceiling.

## v0.1.19  ·  2026-08-31 10:10 PM ET

Toolchain aligned with MadGolf: ship.py is now the single gate. It runs audit.py and the test harness and refuses to ship unless both pass, printing the exact upload list on success. audit.sh was rewritten as audit.py in MadGolf's shape — takes an index.html path, prints tick or cross per check, exits nonzero on any failure — and gained checks for BUILD_TIME being in Eastern format, sw.js parsing and honouring SKIP_WAITING, every asset being precached, the update handshake on the page, a maskable icon in the manifest, both data payloads parsing and holding the keys the app reads, and both lock files existing. bump.py now cuts the lock files itself from the working copies it just wrote rather than leaving that to be remembered by hand, locks sw.js alongside index.html as MadGolf does, deletes the superseded pair, and prints the upload list plus a pointer to ship.py.

## v0.1.18  ·  2026-08-31 10:08 PM ET

Changelog split so the header cannot bloat. bump.py now writes the headline — the first sentence, capped at 150 characters — to the CHANGELOG block in index.html, and the entry in full to a new CHANGELOG.md, from one argument. The header keeps the ten most recent versions and rolls the rest into CHANGELOG.md with a pointer line, so the file opens on code rather than on history. The seventeen entries already written were backfilled into CHANGELOG.md at full length and the header trimmed to headlines, so this applies retroactively rather than only to future bumps. Three audit checks added: the header carries a headline for this version, CHANGELOG.md carries the full entry, and the header holds no more than ten.

## v0.1.17  ·  2026-08-31

Installable with auto-updates, on MadGolf's pattern. The page checks for a new service worker on every load rather than waiting out the browser's 24-hour delay, checks again hourly, and hands the waiting worker a SKIP_WAITING message the moment nothing is half-finished on screen — the analogue of MadGolf's mid-round guard here is an open modal or a form field with typing in it. When it is busy it toasts and polls every twenty seconds instead of leaving the update stranded until the next cold start, then reloads once on controllerchange. Home carries an Install button where Chrome offers one and iOS instructions where it does not, since Safari never fires beforeinstallprompt; both disappear once the app is running standalone. Manifest completed with id, scope, description, orientation, categories and a maskable icon. Caching split by what it holds: the shell is cache-first because it only changes on a bump, while data.json and map.json are network-first with the cache as fallback — this app is used at home and in shops rather than on the back nine, so a current shelf beats an offline one. Fault tolerance where it actually bites: localStorage is probed once at startup so private browsing degrades to a session rather than throwing, each saved key is validated for shape and discarded individually if unreadable so one corrupt entry cannot take the shelf down, a failed save says storage is full instead of silently losing an edit, a failed data load explains what to check, and an unhandled render error toasts rather than leaving a blank screen. Eight new audit checks cover the update handshake, the install hook, the maskable icon and the storage probe.

## v0.1.16  ·  2026-08-31

Shop tab added. Type what you are holding in the store: it searches the shelf for anything by that name and shows how many you own, open or sealed, and what you paid. Then it judges the bottle against the shelf from your own data — no lookup service, works offline. Findings cover whether you already own it and whether a sealed backup fits your stocking rule, whether the distillery, category, region or finish would be new, how crowded that corner already is (same category within five proof points), whether it would form a matched pair with something you own at the same house and within one proof point, its price band against your median for that category, and its release tier. A one-line verdict sits on top, counted from the findings so the reasoning stays on screen. Bought it? One tap adds it — a sealed spare if you already own it, a new open bottle if you do not, with the price recorded as paid. Two defects the real shelf exposed and fixed in the same pass: a naive plural produced 'scotchs' and 'irishs', and owning a bottle already was being counted as a point in favour so Ardbeg 10 read as adding something the shelf lacks; owning it now settles the verdict before any other finding is counted. Nav went to seven slots and Reference shortened to Info. +30 tests.

## v0.1.15  ·  2026-08-31

Reference gained two more bodies of content behind a group switcher, so it is one nav slot rather than a seventh tab. Tasting (12 terms): what each of the five sheet columns is actually testing — color and why a darker dram is not older or stronger given E150a, nosing without numbing yourself, palate, finish, and why perceived heat is not proof — plus how this shelf runs a blind flight: ascending proof, unpeated before peated, one variable, spotted controls, write before you talk, water added at the same point by everyone, six pours as the fatigue ceiling, one glass shape for the room. Whiskey (25 terms): mashbill and what each grain brings, malting and where peat smoke actually enters, pot against column and why every category caps distillation proof, single pot still; then the barrel — toasting adds flavor, charring adds and subtracts, char levels through alligator, new versus used oak, maturation and why three Kentucky years are not three Speyside years, angel's share, cask size as faster not better; finishing, the four sherry styles, what double/triple/quarter cask do not tell you, STR, single barrel against small batch, solera; and the bottle — cask strength, chill filtration and why non-chill-filtered travels with higher strength, Bottled in Bond, no age statement, independent bottlers and the Williamson name. Searching spans all three groups and labels each hit with its group; browsing shows the chosen group. 73 terms total. +18 tests.

Reference tab added: every attribute the app stores, defined, split by who defines it — categories set in law (bourbon, rye, wheat, Tennessee, American single malt, Scotch, Irish, Canadian, tequila) each cite their standard of identity; Japanese whisky sits under trade-defined because the JSLMA rules are voluntary association labelling standards, not law; then the Scotch regions, what the shelf records (product versus bottle, open/sealed/gone, exit reasons, proof, age, finish, wine cask), our own conventions stated plainly as ours (recognition, release, occasion, the honestly-renamed Ohio retail label, premium, the stocking rule) and the flight vocabulary. Searchable across term, definition and source.
